String topology and graph cobordisms
Andrea Bianchi

Abstract
. We introduce a symmetric monoidal ∞ \infty -category GrCob \mathrm{GrCob} of graph cobordisms between spaces, and use the homology of its morphism spaces to define string operations. Precisely, for an E ∞ E_{\infty} -ring spectrum R R and an oriented d d -dimensional R R -Poincaré duality space M M , we construct a “graph field theory” GFT M \mathrm{GFT}_{M} , i.e. a symmetric monoidal functor from a suitable R R -linearisation of GrCob op \mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}} to the category Mod R \mathrm{Mod}_{R} of R R -modules in spectra; the graph field theory takes an object X ∈ GrCob op X\in\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}} , i.e. a space, to the R R -module Σ + ∞ ​ map ​ ( X , M ) ⊗ R \Sigma_{+}^{\infty}\mathrm{map}(X,M)\otimes R of R R -chains on the mapping space from X X to M M ; by selecting suitable graph cobordisms we recover the basic string operations given by restriction, cross product with the fundamental class, and the Chas–Sullivan operations. The construction is natural with respect to oriented homotopy equivalences of R R -Poincaré duality spaces; in particular, restricting to the endomorphisms of ∅ ∈ GrCob op \emptyset\in\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}} , we obtain characteristic classes of R R -oriented M M -fibrations parametrised by the suitably twisted homology of 𝐁 ​ Out ​ ( F n ) \mathbf{B}\mathrm{Out}(F_{n}) , recovering results of Berglund and Barkan–Steinebrunner. Finally, we describe explicitly the morphism spaces in GrCob \mathrm{GrCob} , answering along the way a question by Hatcher. This allows us to construct a symmetric monoidal functor from the open-closed cobordism ∞ \infty -category 𝒪 ​ 𝒞 \mathcal{OC} to GrCob \mathrm{GrCob} . Composing with GFT M \mathrm{GFT}_{M} , we obtain an open-closed field theory with values in Mod R \mathrm{Mod}_{R} , attaining values Σ + ∞ ​ L ​ M ⊗ R \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}LM\otimes R and Σ + ∞ ​ M ⊗ R \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}M\otimes R at the circle and at the interval, respectively. We expect this to recover and extend constructions of Cohen, Godin and others.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification

18B10, 18N55, 18D20 20E06, 57P10, 55P50

1.
Introduction

1.1.
String topology and string operations

In the first part of the introduction, we let
R
be a discrete commutative ring for (singular) homology coefficients, and we let
M
be a smooth, closed,
R
-oriented manifold of dimension
d\geq 0
.

String topology, introduced by Chas and Sullivan
[
14
]
, is mostly concerned with the study of the free loop space
LM=\mathrm{map}(S^{1},M)
:
the topology of this space is related to the study of closed geodesics for a Riemannian metric on
M

[
58
,
36
]
, and to the symplectic homology of the Liouville domain
T^{*}M

[
73
,
74
,
75
,
1
,
66
,
51
,
2
]
.
In this article we study more generally mapping spaces
M^{X}=\mathrm{map}(X,M)
, for arbitrary spaces
X
, and their homology with
R
-coefficients.

The collection of the homology groups
H_{*}(M^{X};R)
, for varying
X
, carries additional algebraic structure, arising in the form of “string operations”
H_{*}(M^{X};R)\to H_{*}(M^{Y};R)
, for suitably related spaces
X
and
Y
. The most basic operations are the following three.

(1)

For each continuous map
Y\to X
of topological spaces, we obtain a map
M^{X}\to M^{Y}
inducing an operation
H_{*}(M^{X};R)\to H_{*}(M^{Y};R)
in homology.

(2)

Let
X
be a space and let
Y=X\sqcup*
be the space obtained from
X
by adjoining a point. Then
M^{Y}\cong M^{X}\times M
, and the cross product with the fundamental class of
M
gives an operation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{*}(M^{X};R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{*+d}(M^{X}\times M;R)\cong H_{*+d}(M^{Y};R).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\times[M]_{R}}

(3)

Let
X
be a space and let
Y=X\sqcup_{\partial I}I
be a space obtained from
X
by glueing an interval
I=[0,1]
along its boundary, via a map
\partial I\to X
. One can then define a string operation
H_{*}(M^{X};R)\to H_{*-d}(M^{Y};R)

in the spirit of Chas and Sullivan
[
14
]
: up to homotopy equivalent replacements, we may assume that
\partial I\to X
is a cofibration, and hence replace
Y
by
X/\partial I
; then
M^{X/\partial I}\hookrightarrow M^{X}
admits an oriented normal bundle of dimension
d
, and we consider the following composite, involving the Thom isomorphism

H_{*}(M^{X};R)\to H_{*}(M^{X},M^{X}\setminus M^{X/\partial I};R)\cong H_{*-d}(M^{X/\partial I};R)\cong H_{*-d}(M^{Y};R).

1.2.
Graph cobordisms

Graph cobordisms generalise simultaneously the situations (1)-(3) in the previous list.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two spaces. A
graph cobordism
from
Y
to
X
is the datum of a diagram of spaces
Y\to W\hookleftarrow X
, together with a finite, relative CW structure on the pair
(W,X)
consisting only of 0-cells and 1-cells.

More precisely, we fix finite sets
V,E
and a map
\sigma\colon E\times\partial I\to X\sqcup V
, we let
W
be the pushout of
(X\sqcup V)\leftarrow E\times\partial I\to E\times I
, and we fix a map
Y\to W
.

Given a graph cobordism
Y\to W\hookleftarrow X
, we can define as follows a string operation
H_{*}(M^{X};R)\to H_{*+d\cdot\chi(W,X)}(M^{Y};R)
:

•

we fist define an operation
H_{*}(M^{X};R)\to H_{*+d\cdot|V|}(M^{X\sqcup V};R)
, where
V
is the set of 0-cells in
(W,X)
, by iterating operations of type (2);

•

we then map
H_{*+d\cdot|V|}(M^{X\sqcup V};R)\to H_{*+d\cdot\chi(W,X)}(M^{W};R)
by iterating operations of type (3);

•

finally, we map
H_{*+d\cdot\chi(W,X)}(M^{W};R)\to H_{*+d\cdot\chi(W,X)}(M^{Y};R)
by an operation of type (1).

It turns out that the operations defined in this way do not depend on the actual cell decomposition of
(W,X)
, but only on the relative homotopy type of the pair
(W,X)
.
In order to prove this, and in order to be able to define “higher string operations”, we would like to assemble the collection of all graph cobordisms from
Y
to
X
into a
moduli space

\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)
. Intuitively, deformations of graph cobordisms (corresponding to paths in
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)
) should arise by changing continously the attaching maps of 1-cells, or the map
Y\to W
; or even by suitably modifying the relative CW structure of
(W,X)
, though without affecting the relative homotopy type of this pair. The main desideratum for the space
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)
is the following:
for a suitable local coefficient system
\xi_{d}^{R}
on
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)
, the homology with local coefficients
H_{*}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X);\xi_{d}^{R})

should give rise to higher string operations
H_{*}(M^{X};R)\to H_{*}(M^{Y};R)
, possibly with homological degree shifts.

Instead of working with a single pair of spaces
X,Y
, we shall introduce an entire category
\mathrm{GrCob}
of
graph cobordisms between spaces
. We will define
\mathrm{GrCob}
as a certain symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category; the reader unfamiliar with
\infty
-categories may think of
\mathrm{GrCob}
as a category enriched in topological spaces. Objects of
\mathrm{GrCob}
are given by spaces; and for spaces
X
and
Y
we will
define

\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)
as the morphism space from
Y
to
X
in
\mathrm{GrCob}
.

We shall also define a local coefficient system
\xi_{d}^{R}
on each morphism spaces of
\mathrm{GrCob}
; these coefficient system satisfy a suitable compatibility condition, so that taking homology of morphisms spaces yields a new category
H_{*}(\mathrm{GrCob},\xi_{d}^{R})
: this is now a symmetric monoidal category enriched in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}})
, i.e. in the category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
R
-modules. The objects of
H_{*}(\mathrm{GrCob},\xi_{d}^{R})
are again spaces, and the morphism object from
X
to
Y
is the graded
R
-module
H_{*}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(X,Y);\xi_{d}^{R})
.

A formulation of the main result of the article (see Corollary
A.1
) is the existence of a symmetric monoidal functor
H_{*}(\mathrm{GrCob},\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}}),
of symmetric monoidal categories enriched in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}})
, that attains the value
H_{*}(M^{X};R)\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}})
at the object
X\in H_{*}(\mathrm{GrCob},\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}
, and whose behaviour on morphism objects generalises the operations (1)-(3).

1.3.
Graph field theories

We will use the language of
\infty
-categories throughout the article, by which we will be able to focus on the essence of the construction, and also to achieve a notable improvement of the above mentioned result:

•

R
will be a generic
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum;

•

M
will be a generic oriented
R
-Poincaré duality space;

•

the above functor will be constructed at the “chain level”, i.e. between suitable
\infty
-categories enriched in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}:=\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{Sp})
;

•

any oriented homotopy equivalence
M\simeq M^{\prime}
of Poincaré duality spaces will yield a natural equivalence between the corresponding functors; in particular, all constructed higher string operations are homotopy invariant.

Remark 1.2
.

All constructions of this article are natural in
R
with respect to maps of
E_{\infty}
-ring spectra; in fact one can also replace
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
by a general presentably symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, and require that
M
be an oriented
\mathcal{C}
-Poincaré duality space. We refrain from discussing this level of generality, and fix
R
once and for all throughout the article.

In most constructions of the article, starting with the very definition of
\mathrm{GrCob}
,
in Section
3
,
we rely on Theorem
2.17
,
a recent result of Barkan–Steinebrunner
[
3
]
.
For
d\in\mathbb{Z}
and for an
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum
R
, we consider the first delooping
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{S})
of the symmetric monoidal space
\mathrm{Pic}(R)\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, and we construct a specific symmetric monoidal functor
\xi_{d}^{R}\colon\mathrm{GrCob}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
, sending a graph cobordism
Y\to W\hookleftarrow X
to an
R
-module which is non-canonically isomorphic to
R[d\cdot\chi(W,X)]
.
This will be our “compatible choice” of coefficient systems on the morphism spaces of
\mathrm{GrCob}
, see Remark
3.11

When
R
is a discrete ring, the coefficient system
\xi_{d}^{R}
on the space
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(X,Y)
agrees with the one associating with a graph cobordism
Y\to W\hookleftarrow X
the (suitably shifted) determinant of the relative homology
H_{*}(W,X;R)
, considered as a finitely generated, free
R
-module: see Proposition
3.12
for details, and Example
3.13
for other instances of the functor
\xi_{d}^{R}
from the literature for
R
discrete.

The previous discussion allows us to define a new symmetric monoidal category, denoted
\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R}
and enriched in the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
: the objects of
\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R}
are still spaces as for
\mathrm{GrCob}
, and we have an equivalence of
R
-modules as follows, giving a description of morphism modules in
\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R}
:

\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R}\ (Y,X)\simeq\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)_{!}\xi_{d}^{R}\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)}\xi_{d}^{R}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}.

Taking homotopy groups of morphism modules, we may define yet another category
H_{*}(\mathrm{GrCob};\xi_{d}^{R})
; the latter is a symmetric monoidal category enriched in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\pi_{*}(R)}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}})
, the category of graded modules over the discrete graded ring
\pi_{*}(R)
. Objects of
H_{*}(\mathrm{GrCob};\xi_{d}^{R})
are again spaces, and the morphism object from
Y
to
X
is the graded module given by th homology with twisted coefficients
H_{*}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X);\xi_{d}^{R})
. The construction of these enriched symmetric monoidal categories is done in Section
5
, following the theory of Gepner–Haugseng
[
32
]
. The main result of the article is the following.

Theorem A
.

Let
R
be an
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum and let
M
be an oriented
R
-Poincaré duality space of dimension
d\geq 0
. Then there is a symmetric monoidal
R
-linear functor, which we refer to as a “graph field theory”

\mathrm{GFT}_{M}\colon(\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}

satisfying the following properties:

(i)

\mathrm{GFT}_{M}
sends a space
X
to
M^{X}\otimes R
;

(ii)

\mathrm{GFT}_{M}
recovers the basic operations (1)-(3) from Subsection
1.1
at the level of homology.

In particular, for any spaces
X
and
Y
we obtain a morphism in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}

(M^{X}\otimes R)\otimes_{R}(\mathrm{colim}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)}\xi_{d}^{R})\to M^{Y}\otimes R.

The construction is natural with respect to oriented homotopy equivalences of oriented
R
-Poincaré duality spaces.

The proof of Theorem
A
up to property (ii) is achieved in Section
5
, relying on several constructions from Section
4
. Property (ii) is then established in Section

6
: this final step will in particular rely on the formalism of
[
10
]
, allowing one to study relative Poincaré duality via parametrised
R
-modules in spectra.

The last statement of Theorem
A
implies in particular that an oriented homotopy equivalence
f\colon M\simeq M^{\prime}
between
R
-oriented closed manifolds induces an
R
-linear symmetric monoidal equivalence
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}\cong\mathrm{GFT}_{M^{\prime}}
evaluating
f^{X}\otimes R
at
X
. This gives in particular an alternative proof of the homotopy invariance of the Chas–Sullivan string product
[
21
,
25
,
37
]
.

Theorem
A
solves
[
40
, Conjecture 3.18]
.
By taking homotopy groups of morphism objects, we obtain the following corollary, dealing with
plain
categories enriched in graded
\pi_{*}(R)
-modules in abelian groups (for
R
a discrete ring, this simplifies to “graded
R
-modules in abelian groups”).

Corollary A.1
.

In the hypotheses of Theorem
A
, there is a symmetric monoidal
\pi_{*}(R)
-linear functor
\mathrm{GFT}^{\prime}_{M}\colon H_{*}(\mathrm{GrCob};\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{\pi_{*}(R)}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}})
sending a space
X
to
H_{*}(M^{X};R)
and recovering the operations (1)-(3) from Subsection
1.1
.In particular, for spaces
X
and
Y
, we obtain higher homology string operations

H_{*}(M^{X};R)\otimes_{\pi_{*}(R)}H_{*}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X);\xi_{d}^{R})\to H_{*}(M^{Y};R).

All these higher string operations are invariant under oriented homotopy equivalences of oriented
R
-Poincaré duality spaces.

We can in fact interpret the homotopy invariance in the last statement of Theorem
A
more systematically as follows. For a given oriented
R
-Poincaré duality space
M
, we can consider the classifying space
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{hAut}^{+}(M)
of orientation-preserving homotopy automorphisms of
M
; see Subsection
2.10
for details. We can then upgrade Theorem
A
to a functor of
\infty
-groupoids

\mathbf{B}\mathrm{hAut}^{+}(M)\to\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes,R}((\mathrm{GrCob}\otimes\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Mod}_{R}).

Postcomposing with the evaluation at the endomorphisms of the monoidal unit
\emptyset
, we obtain the following corollary, which is originally due Berglund in the case
R=\mathbb{Q}
, and to Barkan–Steinebrunner in the general case, see Remark
1.3

Corollary A.2

(Berglund, Barkan–Steinebrunner)
.

In the hypotheses of Theorem
A
there is a natural
R
-linear pairing

(\mathbf{B}\mathrm{hAut}^{+}(M)\otimes R)\otimes_{R}(\mathrm{colim}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)}\xi_{d}^{R})\to R.

Applying the hom-tensor adjunction and then passing to homotopy groups, we obtain a
\pi_{*}(R)
-linear map

H_{*}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(\emptyset,\emptyset);\xi_{d}^{R})\to H^{*}(\mathbf{B}\mathrm{hAut}^{+}(M);R).

To interpret concretely the result of Corollary
A.2
, we anticipate that
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)
contains, for all
n\geq 0
, a connected component that is homotopy equivalent to
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Out}(F_{n})
, the classifying space of the group of outer automorphism of a free group of rank
n
; this will follow from the combination of Corollary
7.12
. Therefore Corollary
A.2
gives in particular a map from
H_{*}(\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Out}(F_{n});\xi_{d}^{R})
to
H^{*}(\mathbf{B}\mathrm{hAut}^{+}(M);R)
.
For
R=\mathbb{Q}
, a similar map

H_{*}(\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Out}(F_{n});\xi_{d}^{\mathbb{Q}})\to H^{*}(\mathbf{B}\mathrm{hAut}_{\partial}^{+}(M\setminus\mathring{D}^{d});\mathbb{Q})

has been constructed in the cases in which
M
is a connected sum of
g\geq 0
copies of
S^{k}\times S^{k}

[
8
]
, or a connected sum of
g
copies of
S^{k}\times S^{l}
with
k\neq l

[
69
]
.

Remark 1.3

(A remark on attributions)
.

Before the beginning of this project, Berglund has announced a generalisation of the results
[
8
,
69
]
: through a
generalized fiber integration map
, he has originally claimed the result of Corollary
A.2
for
R=\mathbb{Q}
and
M
simply connected
[
7
]
. Slightly thereafter, Barkan–Steinebrunner
[
3
]
have claimed the entire statement of Corollary
A.2
; their proof is quite parallel to ours, the main difference being that we obtain Corollary
A.2
as an immediate corollary of Theorem
A
, while Barkan–Steinebrunner prove it directly.

In particular, the map
(\mathbf{B}\mathrm{hAut}^{+}(M)\otimes R)\otimes_{R}(\mathrm{colim}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)}\xi_{d}^{R})\to R
constructed by Barkan–Steinebrunner agrees with ours, and so do the characteristic classes of oriented
R
-Poincaré fibrations obtained from the twisted homology of
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Out}(F_{n})
. Barkan–Steinebrunner are also able to prove that their characteristic classes agree with Berglund’s ones (when the latter are defined) in even degrees; conjecturally this should hold in all degrees.

1.4.
Morphism spaces in
\mathrm{GrCob}
and open-closed field theories

The statement of Theorem
A
invites us to understand the homotopy type of the morphism spaces of
\mathrm{GrCob}
; as this
\infty
-category is introduced as a localisation of another
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
, the description of morphism spaces is not immediate.
We will give in Section
7
a relatively simple description of morphism spaces in
\mathrm{GrCob}
which genuinely resembles Definition
1.1
; building on this, we will in particular prove the following theorem.

Theorem B
.

Let
\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
denote the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of cospans of spaces.
Then the canonical functor
D_{0}\colon\mathrm{GrCob}\to\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
(see Remark
3.7
for details)
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

the induced map on groupoid cores
\mathrm{GrCob}^{\simeq}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})^{\simeq}
is an equivalence; in particular we have
\mathrm{GrCob}^{\simeq}\simeq\mathcal{S}^{\simeq}
;

(2)

for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{S}
with
X
aspherical (i.e.
\pi_{i}(X)=0
for all
i\geq 2
and all choices of basepoint), the induced map on morphism spaces

\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)\to\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})(Y,X)

exhibits the source as the subspace of the target spanned by those cospans
Y\to W\leftarrow X
such that
W
can be obtained from
X
by attaching finitely many cells of dimension at most 1.

In particular
D_{0}
restricts to an inclusion of
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{GrCob}_{\mathrm{asph}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
, where
\mathrm{GrCob}_{\mathrm{asph}}\subseteq\mathrm{GrCob}
denotes the symmetric monoidal full
\infty
-subcategory spanned by aspherical spaces.

Theorem
B
is the combination of Corollary
7.10
and Proposition
7.24
.

Remark 1.4
.

For
X\in\mathcal{S}
, the space
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(\emptyset,X)
was considered by Hatcher in a talk given in 2012
[
39
]
, via a different but equivalent construction. In this talk Hatcher asked a question that can be rephrased as follows: for which
X
does
D_{0}
induce an inclusion
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(\emptyset,X)\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{S}_{X/})^{\simeq}
on morphism spaces? Theorem
B
shows that this is the case when
X
is aspherical; we will see instead in Example
7.15
that
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(\emptyset,X\to(\mathcal{S}_{X/})^{\simeq}
is not an inclusion of spaces if
X=S^{k}
is a sphere of dimension
k\geq 2
, and we believe that the argument in this example can be generalised to any non-aspherical space. This solves Hatcher’s question, notably in the positive for aspherical spaces. This also shows, however, that the entire functor
D_{0}\colon\mathrm{GrCob}\to\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})^{\simeq}
is not an inclusion of
\infty
-categories.

Remark 1.5
.

As a simple and immediate application of Theorem
B
, we consider the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial}
of cobordisms between oriented compact 1-manifolds with boundary, where cobordisms are oriented surfaces with boundary and corners. We further select the wide symmetric monoidal
\infty
-subcategory
\mathcal{OC}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial}
spanned by cobordisms whose handle dimension relative to the outgoing boundary is at most 1.
1
1

1

This condition is usually expressed by requiring that each component of the cobordism must have some incomining or free boundary.

The
\infty
-category
\mathcal{OC}
has been extensively studied in the context of string topology and higher operations on Hochschild complexes of Frobenius algebras; an incomplete list of sources includes
[
23
,
34
,
45
,
80
,
78
,
4
]
. According to Costello
[
23
]
,
\mathcal{OC}
was first introduced by Segal
[
67
]
and Moore
[
59
]
.

The canonical symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functor
\mathcal{OC}\to\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
, sending a cobordism of manifolds to the underlying cospan of spaces, evidently factors through
\mathrm{GrCob}_{\mathrm{asph}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, thus giving rise to a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functor
\mathcal{OC}\to\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Composing the latter with
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}
, we obtain an open-closed field theory
\mathcal{OC}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
attaining values
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}LM\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}R
and
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}M\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}R
at the circle and the interval, respectively.
This connection justifies our use of the terminology of “graph cobordism” and “graph field theory”, which we take from
[
40
]
.
The above open-closed field theory allows us to define string operations using the homology of moduli spaces of Riemann surfaces with boundary; we expect such operations to agree with analogous ones constructed so far in the literature
[
20
,
19
,
76
,
15
,
77
,
34
,
63
,
26
,
4
]
.

1.5.
Related work

The results of this article generalise and complement other results in the literature; we make here a short and necessarily incomplete summary.

Classifying spaces of Lie groups:

Hepworth–Lahtinen
[
40
]
define similar category of graph cobordism, whose objects are graphs. They associate a “graph field theory” with a compact Lie group
G
, attaining the value
H_{*}(\mathrm{map}(X,\mathbf{B}G);\mathbb{F}_{2})
at the graph
X
. Our category
\mathrm{GrCob}
is a variation of theirs. The main (parallel) differences are the following:

•:

we consider mapping spaces into
M
, while
[
40
]
consider mapping spaces into
\mathbf{B}G
; hence in their case it is the loop space of the target space, and not the target space itself, to be a Poincaré duality space.

•:

in our graph cobordisms
Y\to W\hookleftarrow X
we attach 0-cells and 1-cells to
X
; in
[
40
]
, only 1-cells seem to be allowed (but we believe that an extended construction allowing 2-cells should be possible in their setting).

String operations for the homology of free loop spaces of classifying spaces of compact Lie groups have also been studied in
[
16
]
.

Homotopy invariance:

The invariance under homotopy equivalences of manifolds for string product and string bracket is proved in
[
21
,
25
,
37
]
. In this article we explicitly recover the invariance of the string product; similar arguments should recover the invariance of the string bracket.

String operations for non-manifolds:

For an
R
-oriented
d
-dimensional Poincaré duality space
M
, Klein
[
48
]
defines an
E_{1}
-algebra structure on
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}(LM)\otimes R[-d]
; this endows in particular
H_{*}(LM;R)
with a string product, coinciding with the one of Chas–Sullivan when
M
is a manifold, and gives an alternative proof of the homotopy invariance of the string product. Over the rationals, string operations for (triangulated) Poincaré duality spaces have been constructed in
[
72
]
. String operations for orbifolds
[
54
]
, Gorenstein spaces
[
27
]
, and differentiable stacks
[
5
,
6
]
have also been constructed.

Homology of moduli of Riemann surfaces:

Higher string operations on the homology of the free loop space of a manifolds have been extensively constructed using moduli spaces of Riemann surfaces, or various versions of chord diagrams; in some cases, lifts of these operations to the spectral/chain level are also provided, as well as generalisations to arbitrary multiplicative (co)homology theories
[
20
,
19
,
76
,
15
,
77
,
34
,
63
,
26
,
4
]

String operations on Hochschild homology:

Operations on the Hochschild (co)homology or the Hochschild (co)chains of (differentiable) graded (commutative, Frobenius) algebras, parametrised by various complexes of graphs and chord diagrams, have been studied by several authors
[
28
,
23
,
70
,
71
,
24
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
80
,
78
]

Remark 1.6

(The string coproduct)
.

Together with the string product, the string coproduct
H_{*+d-1}(LM,M)\to H_{*}((LM,M)^{2})
introduced by Goresky–Hingston
[
35
]
is arguably the most studied string operation in the literature. The string coproduct can be made into an non-relative operation
H_{*+d-1}(LM)\to H_{*}(LM^{2})

[
41
]
. It has originally been defined under the assumption that
M
is a closed manifold, but it can in fact be defined also when
M
is an oriented homology manifold
[
65
]
, and even when
M
is an oriented Poincaré duality spaces endowed with a refinement of the diagonal
\Delta\colon M\to M\times M
to a Poincaré embedding, by a straightforward generalisation of
[
62
, Section 4.3]
. Naef
[
60
]
has proved that the string coproduct is in general
not
invariant under homotopy equivalences of manifolds; see also the discussion in
[
61
]
. We mention however that the string coproduct is invariant under
simple
homotopy equivalences
[
62
,
46
]
; see also
[
79
]
.
Combining Naef’s result with the last statement of Theorem
A
, we deduce that
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}

cannot possibly recover the string coproduct
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we recollect most of the background material that we will use throughout the article. Some of the material has appeared in essentially the same form in
[
9
]
, and is included here for the sake of completeness. We encourage the reader to proceed directly to the next section on a first reading of the article.

We fix three nested Grothendieck universes

\mathfrak{U}_{1}\subsetneq\mathfrak{U}_{2}\subsetneq\mathfrak{U}_{3}
,
and we refer to objects whose size is bounded above by
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{U}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{U}_{3}
as “small”, “large” and “huge”, respectively.
For
i=1,2
, an
\infty
-category is
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
-presentable
if it is
\mathfrak{U}_{i+1}
-small, admits
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
-small colimits, and is
\kappa
-accessible for some regular cardinal
\kappa<\mathfrak{U}_{i}
. We abbreviate “
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-presentable” by “presentable”.

2.1.
Spaces and
\infty
-categories

For
i=1,2
we denote by
\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
the
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
-presentable
\infty
-category of
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
-small
\infty
-groupoids; for
i=1
we write
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{1}}
and refer to it as the
\infty
-category of “spaces”.
We will sometimes consider
\mathrm{Top}
, the (ordinary) category of compactly generated weakly Hausdorff topological spaces with
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small underlying set, and use “topological space”, as opposed to just “space”, to denote an object of
\mathrm{Top}
.

For
i=1,2
we denote by
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
the
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
-presentable
\infty
-category of
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
-small
\infty
-categories. For
i=1
we abbreviate
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}=\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{1}}
.
The fully faithful inclusion
\mathcal{S}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
has a left adjoint
|-|\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}\to\mathcal{S}
, called the
classifying space functor
, and a right adjoint
(-)^{\simeq}\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}\to\mathcal{S}
, called the
core groupoid functor
.

For
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
we denote by
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
the (small)
\infty
-category of functors from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
and natural transformations; it is the internal hom in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
, which is cartesian closed. The morphism
space
from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
is recovered as the core groupoid
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{\simeq}
.

For
i=1,2
we denote by
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})
the
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
-presentable
\infty
-category of
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
-small symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories, with morphisms given by symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functors. This can be equivalently defined as the category of commutative monoid objects
[
57
, Definition 2.4.2.1]
, or of algebras over the
E_{\infty}
-operad, in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
: for this we use that
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
is considered as a
cartesian
symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category: see
[
57
, Proposition 2.4.2.5]
. We abbreviate
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty})=\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{1}})
.

For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})
we usually denote by
\mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{C}
the monoidal unit and we denote by
-\otimes-\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
the monoidal product.
When the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}
comes from a categorical coproduct, either on
\mathcal{C}
or on a closely related
\infty
-category, we will instead use the notations
\emptyset
and
-\sqcup-
for the monoidal unit and the monoidal product of
\mathcal{C}
.
For
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})
we let
\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{\simeq}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
denote the corresponding morphism space in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})
.

We denote by
\mathrm{Fin}
the category of finite sets;
we regard
\mathrm{Fin}
as a full
\infty
-subcategory of
\mathcal{S}
.
For
k\geq 0
we denote by
\underline{k}\in\mathrm{Fin}
the finite set
\left\{1,\dots,k\right\}
. We have
\emptyset=\underline{0}
.
We usually endow
\mathrm{Fin}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
with the coproduct symmetric monoidal structure; when
\mathcal{S}
is endowed with the product symmetric monoidal structure, we stress this by writing
(\mathcal{S},\times)
.

We denote by
[1]
the category given by the linearly ordered set with two elements
0<1
; for an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
we denote by
d_{1},d_{0}\colon\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
the source and target functor, respectively.

2.2.
Presheaves

For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
we let
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}):=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{S})
denote the category of space-valued presheaves over
\mathcal{C}
, and we denote by
\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
the Yoneda embedding. For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty})
, the category
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
is endowed with a symmetric monoidal structure
-\otimes_{\mathrm{Day}}-
given by
Day convolution
, which can be characterised by the following properties
[
57
, Corollary 4.8.1.12]
:

•

the Yoneda embedding can be promoted to a symmetric monoidal functor;

•

the monoidal product
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
preserves colimits separately in each variable.

These properties give rise to the following formula: for objects
A\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{i\in I}\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!(x_{i})
and
B\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{j\in J}\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!(x^{\prime}_{j})
in
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
, written as colimits of representable presheaves, we have an equivalence

A\otimes_{\mathrm{Day}}B\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!(x_{i}\otimes x^{\prime}_{j}).

A functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
induces a functor
F^{*}\colon\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
by restriction, with left adjoint
F_{!}\colon\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
given by left Kan extension. The functor
F_{!}
is symmetric monoidal, whereas
F^{*}
is in general only lax symmetric monoidal.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{D}
is a
presentably symmetric monoidal

\infty
-category, i.e.
\mathcal{D}
is
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-presentable and the monoidal product
\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
preserves
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small colimits in each variable separately.
Then we may fix a regular cardinal
\kappa<\mathfrak{U}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{D}
is
\kappa
-accessible, set
\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{D}^{\kappa}\subset\mathcal{D}
for the (
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small) full symmetric monoidal
\infty
-subcategory spanned by
\kappa
-compact objects, and consider the (still fully faithful) restricted Yoneda embedding
\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!_{\kappa}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
. The functor
\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!_{\kappa}
is still endowed with a symmetric monoidal structure, and moreover it preserves all limits and
\kappa
-filtered colimits, in particular it is accessible. By
[
55
, Corollary 5.5.2.9]
we have that
\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!_{\kappa}
admits a left adjoint

\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!^{\prime}_{\kappa}\colon\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{D}
, which is given by the following formula: if
A\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{i\in I}\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!(x_{i})\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa})
, then
\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!^{\prime}_{\kappa}(A)\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{i\in I}x_{i}
.

From this and the formula for
A\otimes_{\mathrm{Day}}B
given above, together with the hypothesis that the monoidal product on
\mathcal{D}
preserves colimits in each variable, we obtain for
A,B\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
as above the chain of equivalences

\begin{split}\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!^{\prime}_{\kappa}(A\otimes_{\mathrm{Day}}B)&\simeq\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\mathrm{colim}_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!_{\kappa}(x_{i}\otimes x^{\prime}_{j}))\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{(i,j)\in I\times J}x_{i}\otimes x^{\prime}_{j}\\
&\simeq(\mathrm{colim}_{i\in I}x_{i})\otimes(\mathrm{colim}_{j\in J}x^{\prime}_{j})\simeq\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!^{\prime}_{\kappa}(A)\otimes\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!^{\prime}_{\kappa}(B),\end{split}

from which we learn that the oplax symmetric monoidal structure on the left adjoint
\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!^{\prime}_{\kappa}
is in fact a symmetric monoidal structure.

2.3.
Localisation of
\infty
-categories

The following material appears in essentially the same form in
[
9
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

We denote by
\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}\subset\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty})
the full
\infty
-subcategory spanned by wide subcategory inclusions
W\to\mathcal{C}
, i.e. functors inducing an equivalence on core groupoids and inclusions on morphism spaces.
An object in
\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
is usually denoted as a pair
(\mathcal{C},W)
, and is called a
relative
\infty
-category
.

The fully faithful inclusion
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
sending
\mathcal{C}\mapsto(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\simeq})
admits a left adjoint
L\colon\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
, sending
(\mathcal{C},W)
to the localisation of
\mathcal{C}
at all morphisms in
W
.

The following is an adaptation of
[
17
, Chapter 7.2]
for
left
calculi of fractions.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},W)\in\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
and let
x\in\mathcal{C}
. A
left calculus of fractions at
x
is a functor
\pi(x)\colon W(x)\to\mathcal{C}_{x/}
satisfying the following properties:

•

the
\infty
-category
W(x)
admits an initial object
x_{0}
, and
\pi(x)\colon x_{0}\mapsto\mathrm{Id}_{x}
;

•

\pi(x)
takes value in the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{x/}
spanned by arrows that are contained in
W
;

•

the left Kan extension functor
(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}\colon\mathscr{P}(W(x))\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
sends the terminal presheaf
*\in\mathscr{P}(W(x))
to a presheaf
(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}(*)\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
that factors through
L(\mathcal{C},W)
, i.e. morphisms in
W^{\mathrm{op}}
are sent to invertible morphisms in
\mathcal{S}
along
(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}(*)
.

We remark that, for any functor
\pi(x)\colon W(x)\to\mathcal{C}_{x/}
and for
y\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}(*)(y)\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{z\in W(x)}\mathcal{C}(y,d_{0}\pi(z))
; we can also consider the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}_{y/}\times_{\mathcal{C}}W(x)
, where the fibre product is taken along the functors
d_{0}\colon\mathcal{C}_{y/}\to\mathcal{C}
and
d_{0}\pi\colon W(x)\to\mathcal{C}
; then we have an equivalence
(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}(*)(y)\simeq|\mathcal{C}_{y/}\times_{\mathcal{C}}W(x)|
.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
17
, Theorem 7.2.8]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},W)
be a relative
\infty
-category, let
x\in\mathcal{C}
and let
\pi(x)\colon W(x)\to\mathcal{C}_{x/}
be a left calculus of fractions at
x
. Then we have an equivalence in
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})

L(\mathcal{C},W)(-,x)\simeq(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}(*).

2.4.
{(\infty,2)}
-categories, deloopings and Gray tensor product

For
i=1,2
we denote by
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i+1}}
the
\infty
-category of
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
-small
{(\infty,2)}
-categories; it is endowed with the cartesian symmetric monoidal structure.
We denote by
|-|_{2}
and
(-)^{2\simeq}\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
the left and right adjoint to the inclusion
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}\subset\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
of
\mathfrak{U}_{i+1}
-small
\infty
-categories, respectively. We further write
|-|
and
(-)^{\simeq}\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
for the left and right adjoint to the inclusion
\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}\subset\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
, respectively.

For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})
we let
\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})
denote the
first delooping
of
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. the essentially unique symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category with an equivalence

\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}},\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}})\simeq\mathcal{C}
and such that
(\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
is a connected space.
Taking the first delooping gives a fully faithful inclusion
\mathbf{B}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)})
with essential image given by the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-categories whose space of objects is connected.
We usually denote by
*=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}}
the monoidal unit of a first delooping, which we regard as a “base object”.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-groupoid, then
\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})
; and if
\mathcal{C}
is moreover group-like with respect to the tensor product, then
\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})
.

For
1\leq i\leq 2
we denote by
\circledast
the Gray tensor product on
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
; it gives
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
a (non-symmetric) monoidal structure, which is
not
equivalent to the cartesian symmetric monoidal structure
\infty
-category. An introduction to the subject can be found in
[
30
, Chapter 10, Section 3]
and in
[
29
]
.

For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
, the endofunctors
-\circledast\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}\circledast-
of
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
admits right adjoints that we denote
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},-)^{\text{l-lax}}
and
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},-)^{\text{r-lax}}
.
Both in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{\text{l-lax}}
and in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{\text{r-lax}}
, an object is an
{(\infty,2)}
-functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
; the difference between the two arises when considering 1-morphisms and 2-morphisms.

For instance, let
F,G
be
{(\infty,2)}
-functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, and let
f\colon x\to y
be a 1-morphism in
\mathcal{C}
; then we obtain 1-morphisms
F(f)
and
G(f)
in
\mathcal{D}
; let now
\alpha\colon F\to G
be a 1-morphism in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{\text{l-lax}}
or in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{\text{r-lax}}
: then
\alpha
produces in both cases 1-morphisms
\alpha_{x}\colon F(x)\to G(x)
and
\alpha_{y}\colon F(y)\to G(y)
in
\mathcal{D}
, together with a 2-morphism filling the following square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(y);}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{y}}
l-lax
r-lax

the difference between the two cases is the orientation of this 2-morphism.

There is a natural transformation
-\circledast-\Rightarrow-\times-
of functors
(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})^{2}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
; evaluating one variable at
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
and passing to right adjoints, we obtain natural transformations
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},-)\Rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},-)^{\text{l-lax}}
and
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},-)\Rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},-)^{\text{r-lax}}
of endofunctors of
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
.

If
\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})
is a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category, then all of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
,
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{\text{l-lax}}
and
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{\text{r-lax}}
are symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-categories by pointwise operations, and the two natural
{(\infty,2)}
-functors
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{\text{l-lax}}\leftarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{\text{l-lax}}
are symmetric monoidal.

Notation 2.4
.

For
1\leq i\leq 2
and for
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
we denote by

d_{0},d_{1}\colon\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{C})^{\text{l-lax}}\to\mathcal{C}

the target and source functors. For
x\in\mathcal{C}
we denote by
\mathcal{C}_{x/}^{\text{l-lax}}
the pullback in
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
of the cospan
*\overset{x}{\to}\mathcal{C}\overset{d_{1}}{\leftarrow}\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{C})^{\text{l-lax}}
, and by
\mathcal{C}_{/x}^{\text{l-lax}}
the pullback of the cospan
*\overset{x}{\to}\mathcal{C}\overset{d_{0}}{\leftarrow}\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{C})^{\text{l-lax}}
. We define similarly
\mathcal{C}_{/x}^{\text{r-lax}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{x/}^{\text{r-lax}}
.
If
\mathcal{C}
is symmetric monoidal and
x=\mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{C}
is the monoidal unit, then all
{(\infty,2)}
-categories
\mathcal{C}_{x/}^{\text{l-lax}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{/x}^{\text{l-lax}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{x/}^{\text{r-lax}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{/x}^{\text{r-lax}}
have an induced symmetric monoidal structure.

2.5.
Stable presentable
R
-linear categories

We denote by
\mathrm{Sp}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
the stable, presentable
\infty
-category of spectra.
We further let
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
denote the large
{(\infty,2)}
-category having stable, presentable
\infty
-categories as objects, left adjoint functors as 1-morphisms, and natural transformations as 2-morphisms. Note that objects of
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}
are only
\mathfrak{U}_{2}
-small as
\infty
-categories (the only exception being
*\in\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}
, which is also
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small); however an
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-presentable
\infty
-category can be described through an
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small amount of data, so the core groupoid of
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}
is
\mathfrak{U}_{2}
-small; similarly, the spaces of 1-morphisms and 2-morphisms in
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}
are
\mathfrak{U}_{2}
-small (in fact even
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small); this justifies our writing
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
.

The
{(\infty,2)}
-category
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}
is endowed with a symmetric monoidal structure, introduced in
[
57
, Section 4.8]
for the underlying
\infty
-category
(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L})^{2\simeq}
, and extended to the
{(\infty,2)}
-categorical setting in
[
30
, part I, chapter 1, section 8.3]
and
[
42
, Section 4.4]
.

We usually denote by
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Sp})
an
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum; this includes the case of a discrete commutative ring
R
, which is identified with its Eilenberg–Maclane
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum.
We denote by
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}=\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{Sp})\in\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}
the
\infty
-category of
R
-modules in spectra. If
R
is discrete,
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is equivalent to the derived
\infty
-category
D(R)
; the category of
R
-modules in abelian groups shall instead be denoted by
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{Ab})
.
For
\zeta\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, we denote by
\zeta[n]
the
n
th
suspension of
\zeta
.

Tensor product over
R
makes
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
into an object in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L})
, i.e. into a
presentably symmetric monoidal stable

\infty
-category. We denote by
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}=\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L})\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}})
the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category of
R
-linear presentable
\infty
-categories, with
R
-linear left adjoint functors as 1-morphisms and
R
-linear natural transformations as 2-morphisms.

We let
\mathrm{Pic}(R)\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\simeq}
denote the full
\infty
-subgroupoid of the groupoid core of
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
spanned by tensor-invertible objects. Invertible modules are in particular dualisable and hence compact, so we have
\mathrm{Pic}(R)\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\omega}
; in particular
\mathrm{Pic}(R)
is equivalent to a
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small
\infty
-groupoid, so we can write
\mathrm{Pic}(R)\in\mathcal{S}
. Tensor product over
R
makes
\mathrm{Pic}(R)
into a group-like symmetric monoidal
\infty
-groupoid, with first delooping
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)\in\mathcal{S}
(see the notation from Subsection
2.4
).

2.6.
Parametrised
R
-modules

We fix
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Sp})
. For a space
X
we denote by
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}=\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
the
\infty
-category of parametrised
R
-modules over
X
, where
X
is considered as an
\infty
-groupoid.
Pointwise tensor product over
R
makes
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
into a symmetric monoidal
R
-linear
\infty
-category, i.e. a commutative algebra object in
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
. We denote by
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X})
the full
\infty
-subgroupoid of
(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X})^{\simeq}
spanned by invertible objects.

Given a map of spaces
f\colon X\to Y
, we denote by
f^{*}=\mathrm{Fun}(f,\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
the induced symmetric monoidal functor
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{Y}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
. It is a morphism in
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
.

Taking categories of parametrised
R
-modules gives a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}:=\mathrm{Fun}(-,\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\colon(\mathcal{S},\times)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})
: in particular, for two spaces
X,Y
, there is a canonical symmetric monoidal and
R
-linear identification
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}\otimes_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{Y}\simeq\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X\times Y}
.

For a map of spaces
f\colon X\to Y
, the functor
f^{*}
admits a left and a right adjoint in
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
, denoted
f_{!},f_{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{Y}
, corresponding to computing colimits and limits on the (homotopy) fibres of
f
. The morphism
f_{!}
is also in
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
, i.e. it is colimit preserving and
R
-linear, whereas the morphism
f_{*}
does not preserve
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small colimits in general, and it only carries a lax
R
-linear structure in general.

Notation 2.5
.

For
X\in\mathcal{S}
we denote by
p(X)\colon X\to*
the terminal map; it induces functors
p(X)^{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
and
p(X)_{!},p(X)_{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
.

For
\zeta\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
we sometimes abuse notation and write
\zeta
for
p(X)^{*}(\zeta)\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
, i.e. the constant parametrised
R
-module over
X
with value
\zeta
.

Notation 2.6
.

We usually denote by
\eta_{f_{*}}\colon\mathrm{Id}\to f_{*}f^{*}
the unit of the adjunction involving
f_{*}
(which is the adjunction between
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
), by
\epsilon_{f_{!}}\colon f_{!}f^{*}\to\mathrm{Id}
the counit of the adjunction involving
f_{!}
, and similarly for
\eta_{f_{!}}
and
\epsilon_{f_{*}}
.

Each
\zeta\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
gives rise to a homology theory on spaces, associating with
X\in\mathcal{S}
the sequence of homotopy groups
H_{*}(X;\zeta):=\pi_{*}(p(X)_{!}p(X)^{*}\zeta)
.
Given a map of spaces
f\colon X\to Y
, we have
p(X)\simeq p(Y)f
and we obtain a map
H_{*}(X;\zeta)\to H_{*}(Y;\zeta)
by taking homotopy groups of the evaluation at
\zeta
of the following composite of natural transformations between endofunctors of
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(X)_{!}p(X)^{*}\simeq p(Y)_{!}f_{!}f^{*}p(Y)^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(Y)_{!}p(Y)^{*}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(Y)_{!}\epsilon_{f_{!}}p(Y)^{*}}

There is also a generalised cohomology theory
H^{*}(X;\zeta):=\pi_{-*}(p(X)_{*}p(X)^{*}(\zeta))
, whose functoriality with respect to maps
f\colon X\to Y
relies on the units
\eta_{f_{*}}
.

Notation 2.7
.

For a natural transformation
\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G
of functors
F,G\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, with
\mathcal{D}
a stable
\infty
-category, we denote by
\mathrm{fib}(\alpha),\mathrm{cof}(\alpha)\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
the functors sending an object
x\in\mathcal{C}
to the fibre, respectively the cofibre, of
\alpha_{x}\colon F(x)\to G(x)
.

Given a map of spaces
i\colon Y\to X
, which we think of as an inclusion, and given
\zeta\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, we can similarly define the relative homology groups
H_{*}(X,Y;\zeta)
as the homotopy groups of
p(X)_{!}\mathrm{cof}(\epsilon_{i_{!}})p(X)^{*}(\zeta)
; similarly, the relative cohomology groups
H^{*}(X,Y;\zeta)
are defined as
\pi_{-*}\big(p(X)_{*}\mathrm{fib}(\eta_{i_{*}})p(X)^{*}(\zeta)\big)
.

If
R
is a discrete commutative ring and
\zeta\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{Ab})\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, then
H_{*}(X;\zeta)
,
H^{*}(X;\zeta)
,
H_{*}(X,Y;\zeta)
and
H^{*}(X,Y;\zeta)
are isomorphic to the usual singular homology and cohomology of
X
and
(X,Y)
with coefficients in
\zeta
.

Homology and cohomology with twisted coefficients are defined by starting with a parametrised
R
-module
\zeta\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
(e.g., a classical coefficient system
\zeta\colon X\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{Ab})\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
) and by omitting the terms “
p(X)^{*}
” in the above formulas.

2.7.
Beck–Chevalley transformations and projection formulae

Given a pullback square of spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

the following standard facts hold:

(1)

by commutativity of the square we have equivalences of functors
\tilde{g}^{*}f^{*}\simeq\tilde{f}^{*}g^{*}
,
g_{!}\tilde{f}_{!}\simeq f_{!}\tilde{g}_{!}
and
g_{*}\tilde{f}_{*}\simeq f_{*}\tilde{g}_{*}
;

(2)

the following composites of natural transformations
are equivalences of functors: we will refer to them as
Beck–Chevalley equivalences
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{g}_{!}\tilde{f}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{g}_{!}\tilde{f}^{*}g^{*}g_{!}\simeq\tilde{g}_{!}\tilde{g}^{*}f^{*}g_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g_{!};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g_{*}\tilde{f}_{*}\tilde{f}^{*}\simeq f^{*}f_{*}\tilde{g}_{*}\tilde{f}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{g}_{*}\tilde{f}^{*},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{g}_{!}\tilde{f}^{*}\eta_{g_{!}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{g_{!}}f^{*}g_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g_{*}\eta_{\tilde{f}_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{g_{*}}\tilde{g}_{*}\tilde{f}^{*}}

and we will usually denote them by writing
\tilde{g}_{!}\tilde{f}^{*}\overset{BC}{\simeq}f^{*}g_{!}
and
f^{*}g_{*}\overset{BC}{\simeq}\tilde{g}_{*}\tilde{f}^{*}
;

(3)

using the previous, we obtain a composite natural transformation as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{!}\tilde{f}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}f^{*}g_{!}\tilde{f}_{*}\overset{BC}{\simeq}f_{*}\tilde{g}_{!}\tilde{f}^{*}\tilde{f}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}\tilde{g}_{!},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{f_{*}}g_{!}f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}\tilde{g}_{!}\epsilon_{\tilde{f}_{*}}}

and we shall also denote this by
g_{!}\tilde{f}_{*}\overset{BC}{\to}f_{*}\tilde{g}_{!}
; this last transformation is in general not an equivalence.

Given a map of spaces
f\colon X\to Y
, we already mentioned that
f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{Y}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
is a symmetric monoidal functor; moreover the first of the following composite natural transformations of functors
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}\times\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{Y}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{Y}
is an equivalence, whereas the second is whenever
f
has compact fibres:

f_{!}(-\otimes_{R}f^{*}(-))\xrightarrow{\eta_{f_{!}}}f_{!}(f^{*}f_{!}(-)\otimes_{R}f^{*}(-))\simeq f_{!}f^{*}(f_{!}(-)\otimes_{R}-)\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{f_{!}}}f_{!}(-)\otimes_{R}-;

f_{*}(-)\otimes_{R}-\xrightarrow{\eta_{f_{*}}}f_{*}f^{*}(f_{*}(-)\otimes_{R}-)\simeq f_{*}(f^{*}f_{*}(-)\otimes_{R}f^{*}(-))\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{f_{*}}}f_{*}(-\otimes_{R}f^{*}(-)).

2.8.
Cospans and spans

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
\infty
-category admitting finite colimits. We denote by
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{C})
the
{(\infty,2)}
-category of cospans in
\mathcal{C}
; we briefly recall the ideas of the construction. Objects in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{C})
are the same as in
\mathcal{C}
. The
\infty
-category of morphisms from
x
to
y
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{C})
is
\mathcal{C}_{(x\sqcup y)/}
; for instance
a 1-morphism
x\to y
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{C})
is a cospan diagram
x\to z\leftarrow y
, and a 2-morphism from
x\to z\leftarrow y
to
x\to z^{\prime}\leftarrow y
is a commutative diagram in
\mathcal{C}
of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y.}

The horizontal composition of 1-morphisms
x\to z\leftarrow x^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}\to z^{\prime}\leftarrow x^{\prime\prime}
is
x\to t\leftarrow x^{\prime\prime}
, where
t
is the pushout in
\mathcal{C}
of the span diagram
z\leftarrow x^{\prime}\to z^{\prime}
.
We usually denote
\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{C}):=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{C})^{2\simeq}
the underlying
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{C})
, in which non-invertible 2-morphisms have been discarded.

The coproduct symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}
gives rise to a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{C})
, in which we take coproducts (in
\mathcal{C}
) of objects, cospan diagrams (pointwise) and morphisms between cospan diagrams. We denote the monoidal product of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{C})
by
-\sqcup-
, even though
x\sqcup y
is usually
not
a categorical coproduct of
x
and
y
in
\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{C})
.
We have two canonical symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{C})
and
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{C})
.
Dually, for an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
with finite limits, we have a
{(\infty,2)}
-category
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathcal{C})
, inheriting a symmetric monoidal structure from the product monoidal structure of
\mathcal{C}
. We denote
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{C}):=\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathcal{C})^{2\simeq}
.

We will use the following fact in the case
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{S}
: we can extend the
\infty
-functor
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}:=\mathrm{Fun}(-,\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\colon\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}

to an
{(\infty,2)}
-functor
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}\colon\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
, sending
X\in\mathcal{S}
to
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
, and sending a span diagram
X\overset{f}{\leftarrow}Z\overset{g}{\to}Y
to the composite functor
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}\overset{f^{*}}{\to}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{Z}\overset{g_{!}}{\to}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{Y}
. This follows from
[
30
, Theorem 3.2.2]
, applied to the setting in which all classes of “horizontal”, “vertical” and “admissible” morphisms (in the terminology from loc.cit.) consist of all morphisms in
\mathcal{S}
; the given theorem is applicable because the Beck–Chevalley transformations from Subsection
2.7
, point (2), are equivalences.

Moreover, since
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}\colon(\mathcal{S},\times)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
is a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-functor, we obtain a symmetric monoidal structure also on the
{(\infty,2)}
-functor
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}\colon\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
.

2.9.
Dualising modules and
R
-Poincaré duality spaces

A space
M\in\mathcal{S}
is a
Poincaré duality space
if it satisfies a suitable form of Poincaré duality, akin to the one enjoyed by closed manifolds. The classical definition of Poincaré duality space of dimension
d

[
11
,
12
,
68
,
81
]
requires a coefficient system
O_{M}\in\mathrm{Pic}(\mathbb{Z})^{M}
, with fibres isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}[-d]
, together with a fundamental class
[M]\in H_{0}(M;O_{M})
, such that for any
\zeta\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Ab})^{M}\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{M}
, cap product with
[M]
induces an isomorphism
H_{*}(M;\zeta)\overset{\cong}{\to}H^{-*}(M;\zeta\otimes O_{M})
.
We will use the following alternative approach based on categories of parametrised spectra, due to Klein
[
47
]
(see also
[
56
, Definition 2]
,
[
52
, Appendix]
,
[
13
, page 93]
and
[
10
]
for more modern accounts); we will adapt the approach to the setting of parametrised
R
-modules.

Let
X\in\mathcal{S}
.
In general
p(X)_{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
does not preserve colimits, and it only carries a lax
R
-linear structure. However, if
X
is a compact space (i.e. it is a homotopy retracts of a finite cell complex), then
p(X)_{*}
preserves colimits and its lax
R
-linear structure is in fact
R
-linear. By the classification of
R
-linear functors known as “Morita theory”, in this case, there is a contractible choice of the following data:

•

a parametrised
R
-module
\omega^{R}_{X}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
, called “dualising module”;

•

an
R
-linear natural isomorphism
p(X)_{*}\simeq p(X)_{!}(-\otimes_{R}\omega^{R}_{X})
.

Definition 2.8
.

We say that a compact space
M
is an
R
-Poincaré duality space of dimension
d\geq 0
if
\omega_{M}^{R}
has fibres isomorphic to
R[-d]
.

A map of spaces
f\colon X\to Y
is an
R
-Poincaré fibration of dimension
d
if its (homotopy) fibres are
R
-Poincaré duality spaces of dimension
d
.

By means of parametrised homotopy theory, if
f\colon X\to Y
is an
R
-Poincaré fibration of dimension
d
then there is a canonical (yet not contractible!) choice of the following data, see
[
10
]
for details:

•

a parametrised
R
-module
\omega^{R}_{f}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
, called “dualising module”;

•

an
R
-linear natural isomorphism
f_{*}\simeq f_{!}(-\otimes_{R}\omega^{R}_{f})
.

Note that if
R\to R^{\prime}
is a map of
E_{\infty}
-ring spectra and
M
is an
R
-Poincaré duality space of dimension
d
, then
M
is also an
R^{\prime}
-Poincaré duality space of dimension
d
, since
\omega_{M}^{R^{\prime}}\simeq(p(M)^{*}(R^{\prime}))\otimes_{R}\omega_{M}^{R}
.

Closed manifolds are examples of
\mathbb{S}
-Poincaré duality spaces. However, there also exist
\mathbb{S}
-Poincaré duality spaces that are not homotopy equivalent to a closed manifold, see for instance

[
33
, Theorem 5.2]
,

[
68
, Sections 7-8]
,

[
81
, Theorems 1.5 and 4.3, Corollaries 5.4.1 and 5.4.2]
.

Definition 2.9
.

An
orientation
on an
R
-Poincaré duality space
M
of dimension
d
is an isomorphism of parametrised
R
-modules
\omega_{M}^{R}\simeq p(M)^{*}(R[-d])
. Similarly, an orientation on an
R
-Poincaré fibration
f\colon X\to Y
is an isomorphism
\omega_{f}^{R}\simeq p(X)^{*}(R[-d])

We remark that an orientation on an
R
-Poincaré duality space
M
yields Poincaré duality isomorphism between
R
-homology and
R
-cohomology of
M
, as we have a chain of equivalences of
R
-modules:

\begin{split}p(M)_{*}p(M)^{*}(R)&\simeq p(M)_{!}\big(p(M)^{*}(R)\otimes_{R}\omega_{M}^{R}\big)\\
&\simeq p(M)_{!}(p(M)^{*}(R[-d]))\simeq p(M)_{!}p(M)^{*}(R)[-d],\end{split}

yielding an isomorphism between the respective homotopy groups.

2.10.
Orientation-preserving equivalences

Let
M,M^{\prime}
be
d
-dimensional
R
-Poincaré duality spaces. Given a homotopy equivalence
f\colon M\overset{\simeq}{\to}M^{\prime}
, we obtain an equivalence of categories
f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{\prime}}\overset{\simeq}{\to}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}
, compatible with the functors
p(M)^{*}
and
p(M^{\prime})^{*}
from
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
; moreover we have an equivalence
f_{!}\simeq f_{*}
obtained by identifying both functors with the inverse of
f^{*}
. We can then write a composite equivalence

\begin{split}p(M)_{*}&\simeq p(M^{\prime})_{*}f_{*}\simeq p(M^{\prime})_{!}(f_{!}(-)\otimes_{R}\omega_{M^{\prime}}^{R})\simeq p(M^{\prime})_{!}f_{!}(-\otimes_{R}f^{*}(\omega_{M^{\prime}}^{R}))\\
&\simeq p(M)_{!}(-\otimes_{R}f^{*}(\omega_{M^{\prime}}^{R}))\end{split}

and by the contractibility of the choice of a parametrised module
\omega_{M}^{R}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}
and together with a natural equivalence
p(M)_{*}\simeq(-\otimes_{R}\omega_{M}^{R})
, we obtain in particular an equivalence of parametrised
R
-modules
\omega_{M}^{R}\simeq f^{*}(\omega_{M^{\prime}}^{R})
.

Suppose now that both
M
and
M^{\prime}
are oriented; then we can write an automorphism of
p(M)^{*}(R[-d])
as the following composite:

p(M)^{*}(R[-d])\simeq\omega_{M}^{R}\simeq f^{*}(\omega_{M^{\prime}}^{R})\simeq f^{*}p(M^{\prime})^{*}(R[-d])\simeq p(M)^{*}(R[-d]).

Definition 2.10
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}
be oriented
d
-dimensional
R
-Poincaré duality spaces. An
orientation-preserving homotopy equivalence
from
M
to
M^{\prime}
is the datum of a homotopy equivalence
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
and a homotopy between the above composite and the identity of
p(M)^{*}(R[-d])
.

More formally, let
\mathrm{Poin}_{d}^{R}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\simeq}
denote the full
\infty
-subgroupoid spanned by
R
-Poincaré duality spaces of dimension
d
; observe that
\mathrm{Poin}_{d}^{R}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\omega}
, in particular
\mathrm{Poin}_{d}^{R}
is
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small and we may regard it as an object of
\mathcal{S}
. Denote by
\mathcal{S}_{/\mathrm{Pic}(R)}
the overcategory over
\mathrm{Pic}(R)\in\mathcal{S}
.

There is a map of
\infty
-groupoids
\omega\colon\mathrm{Poin}_{d}^{R}\to(\mathcal{S}_{/\mathrm{Pic}(R)})^{\simeq}
, given by endowing an
R
-Poincaré duality space
M
, up to contractible choice, with an invertible parametrised
R
-module
\omega_{M}^{R}
and a natural equivalence
p(M)_{*}\simeq p(M)_{!}(-\otimes_{R}\omega_{M}^{R})
, and by then forgetting the second datum, namely the natural equivalence. Informally, the functor
\omega
sends
M
to the arrow
\omega_{M}^{R}\colon M\to\mathrm{Pic}(R)
.
Combining this with the essentially unique functor
\mathrm{Poin}_{d}^{R}\to(\mathcal{S}_{/*})^{\simeq}
sending
M
to
M\to*
, we obtain a functor of
\infty
-groupoids

\omega^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{Poin}_{d}^{R}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\partial\Delta^{2},\mathcal{S})^{\simeq},

sending
M
to the functor
\omega^{\prime}(M)\colon\partial\Delta^{2}\to\mathcal{S}
which in turn sends
0\mapsto M
,
1\mapsto*
,
2\mapsto\mathrm{Pic}(R)
,
(0\to 1)\mapsto(M\to*)
,
(0\to 2)\mapsto(\omega_{M}^{R}\colon M\to\mathrm{Pic}(R))
, and
(1\to 2)\mapsto(R[-d]\colon*\to\mathrm{Pic}(R))

Definition 2.11
.

We define the
\infty
-groupoid of oriented
R
-Poincaré duality spaces

\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}
as the fibre product in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
of the cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Poin}_{d}^{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\partial\Delta^{2},\mathcal{S})^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{2},\mathcal{S})^{\simeq}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{\prime}}
restrict

An object in
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}
is thus a commutative triangle of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Pic}(R),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{M}^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R[-d]}

which is the same as the datum of an oriented
d
-dimensional
R
-Poincaré duality space.
A morphism in
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}
is precisely an orientation-preserving equivalence as in Definition
2.10
.

We observe that the functor
\omega^{\prime}
takes value in
\mathrm{Fun}(\partial\Delta^{2},\mathcal{S}^{\kappa})
for any regular cardinal
\kappa<\mathfrak{U}_{1}
such that
\mathrm{Pic}(R)
is
\kappa
-compact: here
\mathcal{S}^{\kappa}\subset\mathcal{S}
denotes the
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small full
\infty
-subcategory of
\mathcal{S}
spanned by
\kappa
-compact objects. We can thus replace both occurrences of “
\mathcal{S}
” by “
\mathcal{S}^{\kappa}
” in Definition
2.11
, thus showing that
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
is in fact
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small, i.e.
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}\in\mathcal{S}
.

Notation 2.12
.

For an oriented
R
-Poincaré duality space
M
we let
\mathrm{hAut}^{+}(M)
denote the loop space of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}
at
M
, and we let
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{hAut}^{+}(M)
denote the component of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}
containing
M
.

Remark 2.13
.

In Definition
2.8
, one could require that
M\in\mathcal{S}
is just
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
-twisted ambidextrous
in the sense of
[
18
]
, instead of compact. The
\infty
-groupoid
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}
will be
\mathfrak{U}_{2}
-small in this case, but not obviously
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small.

2.11.
Commutative Frobenius algebras

We start by recalling some material from
[
57
, Subsection 4.6.5]
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, and let
x\in\mathcal{C}
be an object. A
duality datum
for
x
is the pair
(x^{\vee},c)
of an object
x^{\vee}\in\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
c\colon x\otimes x^{\vee}\to\mathbf{1}
satisfying the following property: there exists a morphism
u\colon 1\to x^{\vee}\otimes x
for which there exist homotopies
\mathrm{Id}_{x}\simeq(c\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{x})(\mathrm{Id}_{x}\otimes u)
and
\mathrm{Id}_{x^{\vee}}\simeq(\mathrm{Id}_{x^{\vee}}\otimes c)(u\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{x^{\vee}})
. In this case we say that
x^{\vee}
is a dual of
x
, and that
x
is dualisable; the maps
c
and
u
are called counit and unit of the duality.

The following is a symmetric monoidal adaptation of
[
57
, Definition 4.6.5.1]
.

Definition 2.15
.

A
commutative Frobenius algebra
in a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
is the datum
(\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{t})
of a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathfrak{I}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
\mathfrak{t}\colon\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})\to\mathfrak{I}(\underline{0})
such that
(\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1}),\mathfrak{t}\circ\mathfrak{I}(\mu))
is a duality datum for
\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})
, where
\mu\colon\underline{2}\to\underline{1}
denotes the unique map in
\mathrm{Fin}
.

A recent result of Barkan–Steinebrunner
[
3
]
allows one to use the datum of a commutative Frobenius algebra in a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
in order to construct a symmetric monoidal functor from a certain
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Gr}
of
graph cobordisms between finite sets
to
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 2.16
.

Recall Subsection
2.8
. We denote by
\mathrm{Gr}
the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-subcategory of
\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
whose objects are spaces homotopy equivalent to a finite set, and whose morphisms are cospans
B\to W\leftarrow A
with
W
homotopy equivalent to a finite cell complex of dimension
\leq 1
.

For
i=1,2
we have a functor
\mathrm{CFrob}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
, associating with a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
the moduli space of commutative Frobenius algebras in
\mathcal{C}
;
see
[
3
]
or
[
9
, Definition 2.15]
for a precise definition.

Theorem 2.17

(Barkan–Steinebrunner)
.

For
i=1,2
, the functor

\mathrm{CFrob}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}},

associating with a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category its moduli space of commutative Frobenius algebras, is represented by
\mathrm{Gr}
. In other words, we have a natural equivalence
of functors
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}

\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathrm{Gr},-)^{\simeq}\simeq\mathrm{CFrob}.

Similarly, the functor
\Omega\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
, associating with a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-groupoid its loop space at the identity element, is represented by
|\mathrm{Gr}|
, i.e. we have a natural equivalence
\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(|\mathrm{Gr}|,-)^{\simeq}\simeq\Omega
of functors
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}})\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
; the latter equivalence is induced by evaluation at the morphism
\underline{1}\to\underline{1}\leftarrow\underline{0}\in\mathrm{Gr}(\underline{1},\underline{0})
.

We refer to
[
9
, Remark 3.2]
for a comparison between the second part of Theorem
2.17
and the work of Galatius
[
31
]
; Jan Steinebrunner made us aware of this connection.

Remark 2.18
.

Given a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functor
\mathfrak{I}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
\mathfrak{t}\colon\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})\to\mathfrak{I}(\underline{0})\simeq\mathbf{1}
, to check whether
(\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{t})
is a commutative Frobenius algebra in
\mathcal{C}
, after setting
x:=\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})
and
c:=\mathfrak{t}\circ\mathfrak{I}(\mu)
, we need to select a morphism
u\colon\mathbf{1}\to x^{\otimes 2}
and prove that there exist both homotopies
\mathrm{Id}_{x}\simeq(c\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{x})(\mathrm{Id}_{x}\otimes u)
and
\mathrm{Id}_{x}\simeq(\mathrm{Id}_{x}\otimes c)(u\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{x})
. However, if we manage to endow
u
with a
C_{2}
-equivariant structure with respect to the trivial
C_{2}
-action on
\mathbf{1}
and the
C_{2}
-action on
x^{\otimes 2}
swapping the factors, then the morphisms
(c\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{x})(\mathrm{Id}_{x}\otimes u)
and
(\mathrm{Id}_{x}\otimes c)(u\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{x})
automatically lie in the same path component of
\mathcal{C}(x,x)
, so that it suffices to prove only the existence of the first, or of the second, homotopy.

An alternative proof of Theorem
2.17
is given in
[
9
]
; in the last work we introduced a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
, which refines
\mathrm{Gr}
in the sense that we have an equivalence of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories
|{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}|_{2}\simeq\mathrm{Gr}
.

3.
Graph cobordisms between spaces and local coefficient systems

In this section we introduce the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{GrCob}
, as well as the symmetric monoidal functor
\xi_{d}^{R}\colon\mathrm{GrCob}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
providing “compatible” local coefficient systems (with values invertible
R
-modules) over the morphism spaces of
\mathrm{GrCob}
. The functor
\xi_{d}^{R}
depends on
d\in\mathbb{Z}
and is natural in
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Sp})
.

3.1.
Two categories of graph cobordisms between spaces

We introduce two symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories of graph cobordisms between spaces
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathrm{GrCob}
. The first is a pullback of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories, the second is a localisation of the first.

Definition 3.1
.

Recall the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Gr}
from Subsection
2.11
. We define
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
(respectively
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}_{\mathcal{S}}
)
as the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category obtained as pullback of the following diagram in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{S}))_{/\emptyset}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Gr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

In order to reinterpret
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
in a more familiar way, we consider the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{S}))
.

Notation 3.2
.

We denote by
D_{0},D_{1}\colon\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{S}))\to\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
the two symmetric monoidal functors induced by
d_{0},d_{1}\colon\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{S}
, respectively.

We note that an object of
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a cospan
A\to X\leftarrow\emptyset
, where
A
is a finite set and
X
is any space: this datum is essentially given by the arrow
A\to X
, i.e. by an arrow of spaces whose source is a finite set; we consider this as a particular kind of object in
\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{S}))
. We further note that a morphism in
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
from
(B\to Y)
to
(A\to X)
is a morphism
G\in\mathrm{Gr}(B,A)

together with a square in the
{(\infty,2)}
-category
\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
as the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset.}

Concretely, the left-bottom composition of the previous diagram, as a morphism in
\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
, is given by the cospan of spaces
B\to Y\leftarrow\emptyset
, whereas the top-right composite is given by
B\to W\leftarrow\emptyset
, where we set
W:=X\sqcup_{A}G
. Using that
\emptyset\in\mathcal{S}
is initial, we obtain the validity of the following notation.

Notation 3.3
.

We usually represent a morphism in
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
from
(B\to Y\leftarrow\emptyset)
to
(A\to X\leftarrow\emptyset)
as a commutative diagram in
\mathcal{S}
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}

The right square in the diagram in Notation
3.3
has the property of being a pushout; if we interpret the same diagram as a morphism in
\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{S}))
,its image along
D_{1}
satisfies the additional property of lying in
\mathrm{Gr}\subset\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
. We obtain the following, which we state as a lemma for future reference.

Lemma 3.4
.

The symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
is equivalent to the wide symmetric monoidal
\infty
-subcategory of
D_{1}^{-1}(\mathrm{Gr})\subset\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{S}))
spanned by morphisms represented by a diagram as in Notation
3.3
whose right square is a pushout square.

Given a morphism in
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
as in Notation
3.3
, we can think of its image along
D_{0}
, i.e. the cospan
Y\to W\leftarrow X
, as a graph cobordism between the spaces
Y
and
X
as in Definition
1.1
: the space
W
is obtained from
X
by attaching the graph
G
, and the space
Y
is endowed with a map to
W
.
The main discrepancy between Definition
1.1
and the above description of morphisms in
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the presence of the finite sets
B
and
A
. To get rid of this “redundant” data, we introduce the second
\infty
-category of graph cobordisms between spaces.

Definition 3.5
.

A morphism in
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
is
idle
if its image along
D_{0}
is an equivalence; concretely, representing such morphism as a diagram as in Notation
3.3
, we are requiring that both maps of spaces
Y\to W\leftarrow X
are equivalences.

Idle morphism form a wide symmetric monoidal
\infty
-subcategory of
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
We let
\mathrm{GrCob}
denote the symmetric monoidal localisation of
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
at idle morphisms.

Remark 3.6
.

Consider a morphism in
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
as in Notation
3.3
.
It is clear that if
A\to G
is an equivalence, then also
X\to W
is an equivalence. Conversely,
if
X\to W
is an equivalence, it is an (integral) homology equivalence; this implies that
A\to G
is an integral homology equivalence, and since
G
is equivalent to a finite cell complex of dimension
\leq 1
, this implies that
A\to G
is in fact an equivalence of spaces.
All in all, we may think of an idle morphism as being just of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

In particular, taking
X=Y
and
B=\emptyset
, we obtain that every object
(A\to X)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
is connected by some idle morphism to an object of the form
(\emptyset\to X)
. This justifies our thinking of objects in
\mathrm{GrCob}
as being just spaces; in fact we will prove in Corollary
7.10
the equivalence of core groupoids
\mathcal{S}^{\simeq}\simeq\mathrm{GrCob}^{\simeq}
.

Remark 3.7
.

By Definition
3.5
, the symmetric monoidal functor
D_{0}\colon\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
factors through a localised symmetric monoidal functor that we shall still denote
D_{0}\colon\mathrm{GrCob}\to\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
. For
Y,X\in\mathcal{S}
, the induced map on morphism spaces
D_{0}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)\to\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})(Y,X)
is easily seen to be an injection on
\pi_{0}
, with essential image spanned by those cospans
Y\to Y\leftarrow X
with the property that
W
can be obtained from
X
by attaching finitely many 0-cells and 1-cells. We will see in Proposition
7.24
that the above map of morphism spaces is in fact a subspace inclusion, provided that
X
is aspherical.

Definition 3.8
.

For spaces
X
and
Y
we define
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)
as the mapping space

\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X):=\mathrm{GrCob}(\emptyset\to Y,\emptyset\to X).

In Section
7
