we will give a more concrete description of the homotopy type of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)
for
X,Y\in\mathcal{S}
.

3.2.
Coefficient systems in the Picard space

In this subsection we define, for an
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum
R
and an integer
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functor
\xi_{d}^{R}\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
. Since
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
is a symmetric monoidal
space
, by Theorem
2.17
we have equivalences of spaces

\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathrm{Gr},\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R))^{\simeq}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(|\mathrm{Gr}|,\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R))^{\simeq}\simeq\Omega\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)\simeq\mathrm{Pic}(R).

Definition 3.9
.

For
d\in\mathbb{Z}
we let
\xi_{d}^{R}\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
denote the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functor corresponding to
R[d]\in\mathrm{Pic}(R)
along the above equivalence.

By abuse of notation we also denote by
\xi_{d}^{R}
both the composite symmetric monoidal functor
\xi_{d}^{R}\circ\mathcal{G}\colon\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
,and the induced symmetric monoidal functor on localisation
\xi_{d}^{R}\colon\mathrm{GrCob}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
; here we use that
all morphisms
in
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
, and in particular idle morphisms, are sent to invertible morphisms in
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
.

Remark 3.10
.

The construction of
\xi_{d}^{R}
is evidently functorial in
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Sp})
; moreover, since
R[d]\simeq R[1]^{\otimes d}
, we have that
\xi_{d}^{R}
agrees with the composition of
\xi_{d}^{1}
and the symmetric monoidal self map of
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
raising morphisms to their
d
th
tensor power.

Remark 3.11
.

For
X,Y\in\mathcal{S}
, the functor
\xi_{d}^{R}
sends
X,Y\mapsto*\in\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
and hence it induces a map of morphism spaces
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(X,Y)\to\mathrm{Pic}(R)
. We interpret the latter as a local coefficient system over the space
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(X,Y)
, valued in invertible
R
-modules. These local coefficient systems are “compatible” with each other in the sense that they assemble into the symmetric monoidal functor
\xi_{d}^{R}
. The homology groups
H_{*}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X);\xi_{d}^{R})
will parametrise the higher string operations from Corollary
A.1
.

If
R
is a discrete commutative ring, then there is a remarkably explicit description of the functor
\xi_{d}^{R}
. Relying on Remark
3.10
, we will focus on the case
R=\mathbb{Z}
; the cases of other discrete rings can be obtained by base-change. The following property of
\mathbb{Z}
will make the discussion simpler: the natural functor
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}}))\to\mathrm{Pic}(\mathbb{Z})
is an equivalence, where the source is the plain groupoid of invertible graded
\mathbb{Z}
-modules in abelian groups, and the target is the
\infty
-groupoid of invertible
\mathbb{Z}
-modules in spectra. Very explicitly, we have an equivalence of
\infty
-groupoids
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathbb{Z})\simeq\coprod_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{B}C_{2}
: the object
\mathbb{Z}[d]
represents the
d
th
component, and has automorphism group
C_{2}
, generated by
-\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}[d]}
. In particular
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(\mathbb{Z})
is a 2-category, all of whose 1-morphisms and 2-morphisms are invertible.

Given
G\in\mathrm{Gr}(B,A)
, we can compute the relative homology groups
H_{i}(G,A)=H_{i}(G,A;\mathbb{Z})
; these groups are finitely generated, free
\mathbb{Z}
-modules, and they vanish for
i\neq 0,1
.
We have functors of
\infty
-groupoids

H_{i}(-,A)\colon\mathrm{Gr}(B,A)\to(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Ab})^{\mathrm{f.g.free}})^{\simeq},\quad i=0,1,

where the target is the core groupoid of the category of finitely generated, free
\mathbb{Z}
-modules in abelian groups. Putting
\mathbb{Z}
-modules in degree 1 gives a functor
(-)[1]\colon(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Ab})^{\mathrm{f.g.free}})^{\simeq}\to(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}})^{\mathrm{f.g.free}})^{\simeq}
, and by composition we obtain two functors
H_{i}(-,A)[1]
, for
i=0,1
.
We may now consider the determinant functor

\det\colon(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}})^{\mathrm{f.g.free}})^{\simeq}\to\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}}))\simeq\mathrm{Pic}(\mathbb{Z}),

sending a finitely generated,
\mathbb{Z}
-graded free
\mathbb{Z}
-module to its maximal non-zero exterior power. By composition we obtain two functors
\det(H_{i}(-,A)[1])
.

We shall consider the functor
\hat{\xi}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}\colon\mathrm{Gr}(B,A)\to\mathrm{Pic}(\mathbb{Z})
given by

\hat{\xi}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}:=\det(H_{0}(-,A)[1])^{\otimes d}\otimes\det(H_{1}(-,A)[1])^{\otimes-d}.

The following observations are similar to
[
34
, Lemma 13]
:

•

if
G\in\mathrm{Gr}(B,A)
and
G^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Gr}(C,B)
, then we have a long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\to H_{1}(G,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(G\circ G^{\prime},A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(G^{\prime},B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}(G,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}(G\circ G^{\prime},A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}(G^{\prime},B)\to 0}

giving rise to an isomorphism of tensor products

\begin{split}\det(H_{1}(G,A)[1])\otimes\det(H_{1}(G^{\prime},B)[1])\otimes\det(H_{0}(G\circ G^{\prime},A)[1])\cong\\
\det(H_{1}(G\circ G^{\prime},A)[1])\otimes\det(H_{0}(G,A)[1])\otimes\det(H_{0}(G^{\prime},B)[1]),\end{split}

which, after taking
d
th
tensor powers and reassembling the terms, can be rewritten as
\hat{\xi}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}(G\circ G^{\prime})\simeq\hat{\xi}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}(G^{\prime})\otimes\hat{\xi}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}(G)
;

•

we similarly have natural isomorphisms
\hat{\xi}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}(G\sqcup G^{\prime})\cong\hat{\xi}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}(G)\otimes\hat{\xi}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}(G^{\prime})
for
G\in\mathrm{Gr}(B,A)
and
G^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Gr}(B^{\prime},A^{\prime})
.

Using that the morphism spaces of
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(\mathbb{Z})
are aspherical, one can easily check that the above assignments give therefore rise to a symmetric monoidal functor of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories
\hat{\xi}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(\mathbb{Z})
. For a generic discrete ring
R
we can then postcompose
\hat{\xi}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with the functor
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
induced by the ring map
\mathbb{Z}\to R
, thus obtaining a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functor
\hat{\xi}_{d}^{R}\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
.

Proposition 3.12
.

For a discrete ring
R
and any
d\geq 0
, there is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functors

\hat{\xi}_{d}^{R},\xi_{d}^{R}\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}).

Proof.

By Theorem
2.17
, it suffices to provide an equivalence in
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\simeq\Omega\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
between the evaluations of
\hat{\xi}_{d}^{R}
and
\xi_{d}^{R}
at the morphism
\underline{1}\to\underline{1}\leftarrow\underline{0}\in\mathrm{Gr}(\underline{1},\underline{0})
: the first evaluation is by computation
\det(H_{0}(\underline{1};R)[1])^{\otimes d}\simeq R[d]
, whereas the second evaluation is by definition
R[d]
.
∎

Example 3.13
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
\bigvee_{n}S^{1}
denote a wedge of
n
circles, and consider the subspace
B\mathrm{hAut}(\bigvee_{n}S^{1})\simeq B\mathrm{Out}(F_{n})\subset\mathrm{Gr}(\emptyset,\emptyset)
, where
\mathrm{Out}(F_{n})
denotes the group of outer automorphisms of a free group of rank
n
. For a discrete ring
R
, Proposition
3.12
allows us to identify the restricted coefficient system
\xi_{d}^{R}
on
B\mathrm{Out}(F_{n})
with the pullback on
\mathrm{Out}(F_{n})
of the sign
C_{2}
-representation on
R[d\cdot(1-n)]
along the composite group homomorphism

\mathrm{Out}(F_{n})\to\mathrm{Aut}(F_{n}^{\mathrm{ab}})\cong GL_{n}(\mathbb{Z})\overset{\det}{\to}GL_{1}(\mathbb{Z})\cong C_{2}\overset{(-)^{d}}{\to}C_{2}.

These representations (which are trivial for
d
even) have played a prominent role in the literature: most notably, the homology of
\mathrm{Out}(F_{n})
with coefficients in
\xi_{d}^{\mathbb{Q}}
is isomorphic to the homology of Kontsevich’ graph complexes associated with the Lie operad
[
49
,
50
,
22
,
53
]
.

4.
Parametrised
R
-modules over a Poincaré duality space

We fix
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Sp})
and an oriented
R
-Poincaré duality space
M
throughout the section. Our goal in this section is to construct symmetric monoidal functors with values in
\infty
-categories that are closely related to
(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
, see Subsection
2.5
. The main input will be the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})
.

4.1.
The functor
\Psi_{R}(M)

In this subsection we construct a commutative Frobenius algebra in the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
; we will denote by
\Psi_{R}(M)\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
the corresponding symmetric monoidal functor.

Notation 4.1
.

We introduce notation for some maps between powers of
M
, induced along
M^{(-)}
by maps of finite sets:

•

We denote by
1
the identity of
M
.

•

We denote by
\tau\colon M^{\underline{2}}\to M^{\underline{2}}
the automorphism swapping coordinates.

•

We denote by
p=p(M)\colon M\to*
the map obtained from
\iota\colon\underline{0}\to\underline{1}
.

•

We denote by
\Delta\colon M\to M^{\underline{2}}
the diagonal map, induced by
\mu\colon\underline{2}\to\underline{1}
;

•

We denote by
\bar{\Delta}\colon M\to M^{3}
the map obtained from the unique map
\underline{3}\to\underline{1}
.

•

We denote by
\delta\colon M^{\underline{2}}\to M^{\underline{4}}=M^{\underline{2}\sqcup\underline{2}}
the map obtained from the fold map
\underline{2}\sqcup\underline{2}\to\underline{2}
, i.e. the map
\underline{4}\to\underline{2}
sending
1,3\mapsto 1
and
2,4\mapsto 2
.

•

We denote by
\partial\colon M^{\underline{2}}\to M^{\underline{3}}
the map obtained from the map
\underline{3}\to\underline{2}
sending
1,3\mapsto 2
and
2\mapsto 1
.

Notation 4.2
.

To simplify notation, we remove all symbols “
\times
” from now on. For instance, we write “
(p\,1)
” for the map
(p\times 1)\colon M^{\underline{2}}\to M
. We also omit the symbol “
\circ
” denoting composition of maps and functors.

Note that
\partial=(\tau\times 1)\circ(1\times\Delta)
, which we write as
(\tau\,1)(1\,\Delta)
, and similarly
\delta=(1\,\partial)(\Delta\,1)
.

Notation 4.3
.

We sometimes abbreviate
R[d]=s
and
R[-d]=\bar{s}
.

Notation 4.4
.

Given a functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
with values in a category of parametrised
R
-modules, and given
\zeta\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, we denote by
F\otimes\zeta
the functor obtained by pointwise tensor product of
F
and the constant functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{X}
with value
p(X)^{*}(\zeta)
.

If
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{Y}
is also a category of parametrised
R
-modules, and if
F
is of the form
f^{*}
,
f_{!}
or
f_{*}
for some map
f
between
X
and
Y
, we will identify
F\otimes\zeta
with
F\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{Y}}\otimes\zeta)
by the projection formula without further mention (in the case of
f_{*}
, we are assuming that
f
has compact fibres).

Notation 4.5
.

Whenever we want to stress an action of
C_{2}
on a finite set, we write the finite set as a coproduct of finite sets of the form
\underline{1}
and
\underline{2}
. For instance, the
C_{2}
-action on
\underline{3}=\underline{2}\sqcup\underline{1}
swaps 1 and 2, and fixes 3.

We have the following commutative diagrams of
C_{2}
-equivariant finite sets and spaces, respectively; the second is obtained from the first by applying
M^{(-)}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{2}\sqcup\underline{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{2}\sqcup\underline{2};}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}_{\underline{2}}\sqcup\iota\sqcup\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}_{\underline{2}}\sqcup\mu}
fold

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\underline{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\underline{2}\sqcup\underline{1}},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\underline{2}\sqcup\underline{2}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})}

We remark that the right square, involving
\bar{\Delta}
and
\delta
, is cartesian, and that the composite
(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})\delta
is homotopic to
1^{\underline{2}}
.

Definition 4.6
.

We denote by
\Psi_{R}(M)\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-functor corresponding, by virtue of Corollary
A.1
, to the commutative Frobenius algebra
(\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi},\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi})
given as follows:

•

We let
\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
be the symmetric monoidal functor classifying
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})
, endowed with the fibrewise tensor product over
R
.

•

We let
\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi}\colon\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}(\underline{1})\to\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}(\underline{0})
be the functor
p_{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
.

The rest of the subsection is devoted to the proof that the data provided in Definition
4.6
indeed yield a commutative Frobenius algebra. For this we shall make use of the

C_{2}
-equivariant functor
\mathfrak{e}_{\Psi}\colon\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}(\underline{0})\to\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}(\underline{2})
given by the formula

(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}p^{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{\underline{2}}}\simeq\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}\otimes_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}.

Lemma 4.7
.

The functors
\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{\Psi}
belong to
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
, i.e. they preserve colimits and their lax
R
-linear structure is in fact
R
-linear.

Proof.

Use Notation
4.3
.
A priori a functor of the form
f_{*}
is only a right adjoint functor and lax
R
-linear, so that neither of
\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{\Psi}
is obviously in
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
. For

\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi}=p_{*}
, we use that
M
is an oriented
R
-Poincaré duality space and identify

p_{*}\simeq p_{!}(-\otimes p^{*}(\bar{s}))\simeq p_{!}\otimes\bar{s}
, where the last two expressions, related by the projection formula, are both evidently compositions of left adjoint functors.
2
2

2

Note that if
M
is any compact space, we can still identify
p_{*}\simeq p_{!}(-\otimes\omega_{M}^{R})
.

For
\mathfrak{e}_{\Psi}=(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}p^{*}
, it suffices to check that
(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}
is in
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
. For this we use that, since
M
is an oriented
R
-Poincaré duality space, the
C_{2}
-space
M^{\underline{2}}
is a
C_{2}
-equivariantly oriented
C_{2}
-equivariant
R
-Poincaré duality space.

More precisely, we can identify
\omega_{M^{\underline{2}}}^{R}
with the tensor product

(p\,1)^{*}(\omega_{M}^{R})\otimes(1\,p)^{*}(\omega_{M}^{R})
in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{\underline{2}}}
;
and we can further identify the latter with
(p^{\underline{2}})^{*}(\bar{s}^{\otimes 2})
, using twice the
R
-orientation on
M
. We get a
C_{2}
-equivariant equivalence

(p^{\underline{2}})_{*}\simeq(p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(-\otimes(p^{\underline{2}})^{*}(\bar{s}^{\underline{2}}))\simeq(p^{\underline{2}})_{!}\otimes\bar{s}^{\otimes\underline{2}},

where
C_{2}
acts on the functors
(p^{\underline{2}})_{!},(p^{\underline{2}})_{*},(p^{\underline{2}})^{*}
by pre/postcomposition with
\tau_{!}\simeq\tau_{*}\simeq\tau^{*}
, and it acts on
\bar{s}^{\otimes 2}
by swapping the factors.

Similarly,
(1^{\underline{2}}\,p)\colon M^{\underline{3}}\to M^{\underline{2}}
is an oriented
R
-Poincaré fibration with
\omega^{R}_{(1^{\underline{2}}\,p)}\simeq(p^{\underline{2}}\,1)^{*}(\omega_{M})\simeq(p^{\underline{3}})^{*}(\bar{s})
, and
(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})\colon M^{\underline{4}}\to M^{\underline{2}}
is a
C_{2}
-equivariantly oriented
C_{2}
-equivariant
R
-Poincaré fibration with

\omega^{R}_{(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})}\simeq(p^{\underline{2}}\,1^{\underline{2}})^{*}(\omega_{M^{\underline{2}}})\simeq(p^{\underline{4}})^{*}(\bar{s}^{\otimes\underline{2}})
.
We can now write a
C_{2}
-equivariant chain of
R
-linear equivalences of lax
R
-linear functors

\begin{split}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}(-)&\simeq(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{*}\big((1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}(-)\otimes s^{\otimes\underline{2}}\big)\simeq(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}(-\otimes s^{\otimes\underline{2}})\\
&\simeq(1^{\underline{2}}\,p)_{*}(-\otimes s^{\otimes\underline{2}})\simeq(1^{\underline{2}}\,p)_{!}(-\otimes s^{\otimes\underline{2}}\otimes\bar{s})\\
&\simeq(1^{\underline{2}}\,p)_{!}(-)\otimes s^{\otimes\underline{2}}\otimes\bar{s},\end{split}

and the last two expressions are evidently compositions of functors in
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
.
3
3

3

Note that if
M
is any
R
-Poincaré duality space, even in the absence of an
R
-orientation we can still identify
(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}(-)
with the analog of the second to last expression in the chain of equivalences, reading
(1^{\underline{2}}p)_{!}(-\otimes(p^{\underline{2}}1)^{*}((\omega_{M}^{-1})^{\otimes 2}\otimes\omega_{M})
.

∎

By virtue of Remark
2.18
, the final
task is to provide a natural equivalence

\beta_{\Psi}\colon((\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi}\circ\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}(\mu))\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}(\underline{1})})\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}(\underline{1})}\otimes\mathfrak{e}_{\Psi})\simeq\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}(\underline{1})}

The target of the natural equivalence can be identified with
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}}
; to understand the source, we recall that
\Delta\colon M\to M^{\underline{2}}
is the map induced by
\mu\colon\underline{2}\to\underline{1}
under the contravariant functor
M^{(-)}
; applying further the functor
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
we obtain the identification
\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}(\mu)\simeq\Delta^{*}
.
Hence, we need to define a natural equivalence
\beta_{\Psi}
of endofunctors of
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}
as follows:

\beta_{\Psi}\colon(p\,1)_{*}(\Delta\,1)^{*}(1^{\underline{3}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{3}}\,\Delta)_{*}(1\,\bar{\Delta})_{!}(1\,p)^{*}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}}.

Definition 4.8
.

We define
\beta_{\Psi}
as the concatenation of the following two natural equivalences
\beta_{\Psi,1}
, on left, and
\beta_{\Psi,2}
, on right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(\Delta\,1)^{*}(1^{\underline{3}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{3}}\,\Delta)_{*}(1\,\bar{\Delta})_{!}(1\,p)^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}(\Delta\,1^{\underline{2}})^{*}(1\,\bar{\Delta})_{!}(1\,p)^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}\Delta^{*}(1\,p)^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!};}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC/\text{s.m.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\,p)\Delta\simeq 1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}\delta_{!}\Delta_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}\Delta_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq?}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{2}}p^{\underline{2}})\delta\simeq 1^{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p1)\Delta\simeq 1}

The first arrow of
\beta_{\Psi,1}
is given by symmetric monoidality of
(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
, by which both functors
(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}(\Delta\,1^{\underline{2}})^{*}
and
(\Delta\,1)^{*}(1^{\underline{3}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{3}}\,\Delta)_{*}
can be identified with the tensor product of three functors
\Delta^{*}\otimes 1\otimes((p^{\underline{2}})_{!}\Delta_{*})
.
The same arrow can be equivalently considered as a composition of two Beck–Chevalley equivalences, whence we have labeled it by “
BC
/s.m.”, where “s.m.” stands for “symmetric monoidality”.
The second arrow of
\beta_{\Psi,1}

is the Beck–Chevalley equivalence given by the following pullback square, which is the image along
M^{(-)}
of a pushout square of finite sets:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\underline{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\underline{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\underline{4}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\,1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\,\bar{\Delta}}

We observe that in the definition of
\beta_{\Psi,2}
, one of the occurring arrows is a Beck–Chevalley transformation going in the wrong direction; the following lemma shows that this 2-morphism, which a priori only belongs to
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
, is in fact an equivalence, so that the entire
\beta_{\Psi}
, in the end, is an invertible 2-morphism.

Lemma 4.9
.

The following Beck–Chevalley transformation is invertible:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}\delta_{!}\Delta_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}

Proof.

Use Notation
4.3
.
The given Beck–Chevalley transformation is the left vertical map in the following commutative square, whose horizontal maps are obtained by combining the Poincaré duality equivalences for
(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})
and
(p\,1)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}\delta_{!}\Delta_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{*}\delta_{!}\Delta_{*}\otimes s^{\otimes\underline{2}}\otimes\bar{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}\otimes s^{\otimes\underline{2}}\otimes\bar{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PD\text{ on }(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})\text{ and }(p\,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PD\text{ on }(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})\text{ and }(p\,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

It then suffices to prove that the right vertical map is invertible. After removing the factors “
s^{\otimes\underline{2}}\otimes\bar{s}
”, the right vertical map fits as left vertical map in the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{*}\delta_{!}\Delta_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\,p^{\underline{2}})_{*}(p\,1^{\underline{3}})_{!}\delta_{!}\Delta_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\,p)_{*}(1\,p\,1)_{*}\partial_{!}\Delta_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\,p^{\underline{2}})_{*}(1\,\Delta)_{*}(p\,1^{\underline{2}})_{!}\bar{\Delta}_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\,p)_{*}(1\,p\,1)_{*}(1\,\Delta)_{*}\Delta_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC/\text{s.m.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC/\text{s.m.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

It suffices again to prove that the right vertical map is an equivalence. This is the Beck–Chevalley transformation corresponding to the left cartesian square in the following diagram; we can combine it with the Beck–Chevalley transformations corresponding to the right and the total cartesian squares, obtaining the commutative triangle on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\underline{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\underline{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\underline{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\,\Delta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\,p\,1)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\,p)_{*}(1\,p\,1)_{*}\partial_{!}\Delta_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\,p)_{*}\Delta_{!}(p\,1)_{*}\Delta_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\,p)_{*}(1\,p\,1)_{*}(1\,\Delta)_{*}\Delta_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}

We conclude by observing that the diagonal and horizontal arrows in the above triangle are equivalences. For the diagonal arrow, we observe that the total cartesian square has horizontal arrows being equivalences, hence also the corresponding Beck–Chevalley transformation is an equivalence. For the horizontal arrow we appeal to symmetric monoidality: we can identify
(1\,p\,1)_{*}\partial_{!}\simeq(p\,1^{\underline{2}})_{*}(\tau\,1)_{*}(\tau\,1)_{!}(1\,\Delta)_{!}\simeq(p\,1^{\underline{2}})_{*}(1\,\Delta)_{!}
, where we use that
(\tau\,1)_{*}
and
(\tau\,1)_{!}
are equivalent to each other (the adjunctions with
(\tau\,1)^{*}
exhibit each of them as an inverse of
(\tau\,1)^{*}
) and that
\tau
is an involution. Up to this replacement, the above Beck–Chevalley equivalence is just the identification of both
(p\,1^{\underline{2}})_{*}(1\,\Delta)_{!}
and
\Delta_{!}(p\,1)_{*}
with
p_{*}\otimes\Delta_{!}
.
∎

4.2.
A unital enhancement of
\Psi_{R}(M)

In this subsection we enhance
\Psi_{R}(M)
to a symmetric monoidal functor
{\hat{\Psi}}_{R}(M)\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}
; see Notation
2.4
for the lax comma category. Recall in particular that an object in
((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}
is an
R
-linear functor between
R
-linear categories
F\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\to\mathcal{C}
, and a 1-morphism between
F\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\to\mathcal{C}
and
F^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is the datum of a functor
G
and a natural transformation fitting in a square as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

The symmetric monoidal structure on
((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}\subseteq(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}
is given by pointwise tensor product in
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
.
We have a symmetric monoidal target functor
d_{0}\colon((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}\to(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
, and
{\hat{\Psi}}_{R}(M)
will be a “unital enhancement” of
\Psi_{R}(M)
in that we will have a symmetric monoidal equivalence
d_{0}\circ{\hat{\Psi}}_{R}(M)\simeq\Psi_{R}(M)
.

Definition 4.10
.

We define
{\hat{\Psi}}_{R}(M)
as the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-functor corresponding, by virtue of Corollary
A.1
, to the commutative Frobenius algebra
(\mathfrak{I}_{{\hat{\Psi}}},\mathfrak{t}_{{\hat{\Psi}}})
given by the following data:

•

We let
\mathfrak{I}_{\hat{\Psi}}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}
be the symmetric monoidal functor classifying the algebra
(p^{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M})\in\mathrm{CAlg}((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/}^{\text{l-lax}})
. Here we use that
(p^{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M})
is a commutative algebra object in
((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/})^{2\simeq}
, and that the canonical functor
((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/})^{2\simeq}\to((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}
is symmetric monoidal.

•

We let
\mathfrak{t}_{\hat{\Psi}}
be the following rectangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{p_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{*}}

The rest of the subsection is devoted to the proof that the data provided in Definition
4.10
indeed yield a commutative Frobenius algebra. Note that, if this is the case, the application of
d_{0}
obviously yields the data
(\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi},\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi})
of the commutative Frobenius algebra from Definition
4.6
.

We shall need the following
C_{2}
-equivariant 1-morphism
\mathfrak{e}_{\hat{\Psi}}\colon\mathfrak{I}_{{\hat{\Psi}}}(\underline{0})\to\mathfrak{I}_{{\hat{\Psi}}}(\underline{2})
, given by the horizontal composition of the following
C_{2}
-equivariant diagram of categories, functors and natural transformations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{\underline{2}\sqcup\underline{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{\underline{2}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{!}\Delta_{*}\Delta^{*}(p^{\underline{2}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\underline{2}}\Delta\simeq p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p^{\underline{2}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{\underline{2}}\simeq(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\Delta_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}}

We remark that not all functors appearing in the previous diagram are necessarily colimit preserving and strictly
R
-linear; nevertheless the functors
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}}
,
(p^{\underline{2}})^{*}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{\Psi}=(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}p^{*}
have these properties (the latter by Lemma
4.7
), and these are the only functors occurring in the rectangle giving
\mathfrak{e}_{\hat{\Psi}}
, which therefore is a well-defined 1-morphism in
((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}\subseteq(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}
.

By virtue of Remark
2.18
, it suffices to construct a homotopy

\beta_{\hat{\Psi}}
connencting the 1-morphisms
((\mathfrak{t}_{\hat{\Psi}}\circ\mathfrak{I}_{\hat{\Psi}}(\mu))\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\hat{\Psi}}(\underline{1})})\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\hat{\Psi}}(\underline{1})}\otimes\mathfrak{e}_{\hat{\Psi}})
and
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\hat{\Psi}}(\underline{1})}
. Concretely, we need to provide a filling of
the following prism, in which all vertices, edges and vertical faces are already
filled via the data provided so far; in particular the back vertical face is
filled with the identity of
p^{*}
.
Moreover, the requirement on
d_{1}{\hat{\Psi}}_{R}(M)
and
d_{0}{\hat{\Psi}}_{R}(M)
forces us to
fill the the top face with the identity of
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
, and the bottom face
with
\beta_{\Psi}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{\underline{5}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{\underline{3}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{(p\,1)_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\underline{3}}(\Delta 1)\simeq p^{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq p(p1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\delta)_{!}(1\Delta)_{*}(1\Delta)^{*}(p^{\underline{3}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\underline{3}}(1\Delta)\simeq p^{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq p(1p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p^{\underline{3}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{(1\,\Delta)_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{3}}p^{\underline{2}})(1g)\simeq 1^{\underline{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{3}}\,\Delta)_{*}(1\,\bar{\Delta})_{!}(1\,p)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{3}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(\Delta\,1)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\Psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

So all that is left to do, in order to complete the definition of
\beta_{\hat{\Psi}}
, is
to fill the interior of the prism, which is equivalent to providing a
filling of the following diagram.

Lemma 4.11
.

The identity of
p^{*}
is homotopic to the cyclic composition in the following diagram in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R},\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M})
, starting on the top left corner; moreover the right vertical arrow is invertible:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(\Delta\,1)^{*}(1^{\underline{3}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{3}}\,\Delta)_{*}(1\,\bar{\Delta})_{!}(1\,p)^{*}p^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(\Delta\,1)^{*}(p^{\underline{3}})^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(\Delta\,1)^{*}(1^{\underline{3}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1\,\delta)_{!}(1\,\Delta)_{*}(1\,\Delta)^{*}(p^{\underline{3}})^{*}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{(p\,1)_{*}}p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\underline{3}}(\Delta\,1)\simeq p(p\,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\Psi}p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p1)_{*}\!(\Delta 1)^{*}\eta_{(1\Delta)_{*}}\!(p^{\underline{3}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{3}}\,p^{\underline{2}})(1\,\delta)\simeq 1^{\underline{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq?}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC\text{, }p^{\underline{3}}(1\,\Delta)\simeq p(1\,p)}

Proof.

We apply the following procedure to replace the given hollow square by a more convenient one: by means of compatible Beck–Chevalley equivalences, we bring the term “
(\Delta\,1)^{*}
” to the right in all the three corners of the above hollow
square in which it appears; we then suitably recombine all terms
“
(-)^{*}
” that have accumulated at the right end of each corner. The result is the
following hollow square, which needs to be suitably filled:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\,\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}p^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(p\,1)^{*}p^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\,1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})_{!}\delta_{!}\Delta_{*}\Delta^{*}(p\,1)^{*}p^{*}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{(p\,1)_{*}}p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\Psi,1}\,p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p1)_{*}\eta_{\Delta_{*}}(p1)^{*}p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{2}}\,p^{\underline{2}})\delta\simeq 1^{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq?}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC\text{, }(p\,1)\Delta\simeq 1}

Note that the right vertical map of the last square is the horizontal composition of the Beck–Chevalley transformation from Lemma
4.9
with
p^{*}
, hence it is invertible.

We next observe that the composition of
\eta_{(p\,1)_{*}}
and

(p\,1)_{*}\eta_{\Delta_{*}}(p\,1)^{*}
is homotopic to
\eta_{((p\,1)\Delta)_{*}}
,
which is homotopic to the composite equivalence
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}\simeq(p\,1)_{*}\Delta_{*}\simeq(p\,1)_{*}\Delta_{*}\Delta^{*}(p\,1)^{*}
, as
(p\,1)\Delta\simeq 1
. This implies that the
left-bottom-right composite in the above square is homotopic to the bottom
composite in the following commutative diagram, whose top composite is by definition the inverse of

\beta_{\Psi,2}\,p^{*}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!p^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p1)_{*}\Delta_{*}p^{*}\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!(p1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}p^{\underline{2}})_{!}\delta_{!}\Delta_{*}p^{*}\!\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!(p1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}p^{\underline{2}})_{!}(1^{\underline{2}}\Delta)_{*}\bar{\Delta}_{!}p^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p1)_{*}\Delta_{*}\Delta^{*}(p1)^{*}p^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p1)_{*}(1^{\underline{2}}p^{\underline{2}})_{!}\delta_{!}\Delta_{*}\Delta^{*}(p1)^{*}p^{*}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p1)\Delta\simeq 1^{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p1)\Delta\simeq 1^{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\underline{2}}p^{\underline{2}})\delta\simeq 1^{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p1)\Delta\simeq 1^{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

∎

Conjecture 4.12
.

In order to prove that the data
(\mathfrak{I}_{\hat{\Psi}},\mathfrak{t}_{\hat{\Psi}})
from Definition
4.10
yields a commutative Frobenius algebra in
((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/})^{2\simeq}
, we have only used that
M
is an
R
-Poincaré duality space, whereas the datum of an
R
-orientation was only useful to simplify some formulas.

We conjecture that the converse holds true: if
M
is a compact space such that the analogous data
(\mathfrak{I}_{\hat{\Psi}},\mathfrak{t}_{\hat{\Psi}})
as in Definition
4.10
is a commutative Frobenius algebra in
((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/})^{2\simeq}
, then
M
is an
R
-Poincaré duality space.

4.3.
The functor
\Phi_{R}(M)

We conclude the section by introducing a variation
\Phi_{R}(M)
of the functor
\Psi_{R}(M)
from Subsection
4.1
.

Definition 4.13
.

Let
M
be a generic space.
The symmetric monoidal
\infty
functor
\Phi_{R}(M)\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
is defined as the composite symmetric monoidal functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}}

where
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}\colon\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})\to(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}

is introduced in Subsection

2.8
.

The next goal is to prove Proposition
4.15
, which provides a more precise connection between
\Psi_{R}(M)
and
\Phi_{R}(M)
.

Notation 4.14
.

We consider the symmetric monoidal functor

\ell\colon\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)\to(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}

classifying the action of
\mathrm{Pic}(R)\subseteq\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
on
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
by tensor product. Composing
\ell
with the functor
\xi_{d}^{R}
from Subsection
3.2
, we obtain a symmetric monoidal functor
\ell\xi_{d}^{R}\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
.

Taking pointwise tensor product in
(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
, we can now consider the symmetric monoidal functor
\Psi_{R}(M)\otimes\ell\xi_{d}^{R}\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
M
be an oriented,
d
-dimensional
R
-Poincaré duality space. Then there is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal functors

\Psi_{R}(M)\otimes\ell\xi_{d}^{R}\simeq\Phi_{R}(M)\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}.

Proof.

We denote in the following by

(\mathfrak{I}_{{\Psi,\xi}},\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi,\xi})
the underlying commutative Frobenius algebra for

\Psi_{R}(M)\otimes\ell\xi_{d}^{R}
, and compare
(\mathfrak{I}_{{\Psi,\xi}},\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi,\xi})
with
(\mathfrak{I}_{\Phi},\mathfrak{t}_{\Phi})
.
We compare the underlying commutative Frobenius algebras of
\Psi_{R}(M)\otimes\ell\xi_{d}^{R}
and
\Psi_{R}(M)
.

•

The underlying symmetric monoidal functor
\mathfrak{I}_{\Phi}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
is the same as
\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}
, i.e. the symmetric monoidal functor classifying
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}
. This is immediate for
\mathfrak{I}_{\Phi}
. For
\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi,\xi}=\mathfrak{I}_{\Psi}\otimes\ell\mathfrak{I}_{\xi_{d}^{R}}
,
we use that the symmetric monoidal functor
\mathfrak{I}_{\xi_{d}^{R}}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
is constant with value
*\in\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
, and composing with
\ell
we obtain the constant functor
\ell\mathfrak{I}_{\xi_{d}^{R}}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
at
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, i.e. the functor classifying the monoidal unit
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
as a commutative algebra. The tensor product of
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}
and
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is then readily identified, as a commutative algebra, with
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}
.

•

Use Notation
4.3
. The functor
\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi,\xi}
is the tensor product in
(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
of the functors
\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi}=p_{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
and
\ell\mathfrak{t}_{\xi_{d}^{R}}=(-\otimes s)\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
; this gives an equivalence
\mathfrak{t}_{\Psi,\xi}\simeq p_{*}(-)\otimes_{R}s
, which by Poincaré duality is equivalent to
p_{!}\simeq\mathfrak{t}_{\Phi}
.

∎

Remark 4.16
.

Since the symmetric monoidal functor
\ell\xi_{d}^{R}\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
is equivalent to the constant functor at
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, the statement of proposition
4.15
is equivalent to a symmetric monoidal equivalence
\Psi_{R}(M)\simeq\Phi_{R}(M)\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}
; we will in fact employ this reformulation in Section
5
.

5.
The functor
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}

In this section we construct the symmetric monoidal
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
-enriched functor
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}\colon(\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
from Theorem
A
, associated with an
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum
R
and a
d
-dimensional oriented
R
-Poincaré duality space
M
.

5.1.
The functors
Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3}

We start by concatenating two symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functors
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}\to(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}
.
The first, denoted
Z_{1}
, is the composite functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{1}\colon{\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\Psi}}_{R}(M)}

obtained by composing the functor
{\hat{\Psi}}_{R}(M)
from in Subsection
4.2
with the functor
\mathcal{G}
from Definition
3.1
. The second, denoted
Z_{2}\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}
, is the pointwise tensor product in
(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}
of the following two composite functors denoted
Z_{2}
and, by abuse of notation,
\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{2}\colon{\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathcal{S},\sqcup)^{\text{r-lax}}_{/\emptyset})^{2\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathcal{S},\times)^{\text{l-lax}}_{/*})^{2\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}}_{/\mathrm{Mod}_{R}})^{2\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq};}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}\colon{\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{-d}^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}

In the composite
Z_{2}
, the first arrow is the the functor
\mathcal{F}
from Definition
3.1
. The second arrow is obtained from the functor
M^{(-)}\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{op}}
by passing to right lax comma
\infty
-categories of cospan
{(\infty,2)}
-categories, and by then replacing “
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{op}}
” back to “
\mathcal{S}
” at the cost of also replacing “
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}
” by “
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}
” and “
r-lax
” by “
l-lax
”. The diagonal arrow is induced from the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-functor
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}\colon\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
from Subsection
2.8
.

The second functor
\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}
involves the homonymous functor
\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}
from Notation
4.14
; the last arrow labeled “
s_{0}
” is induced by the degeneracy
s^{0}\colon[1]\to[0]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
.

We next compare the composites
d_{0}Z_{1}
and
d_{1}(Z_{2}\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R})
. By the fact that
{\hat{\Psi}}_{R}(M)
is a “unital enhancement” of
\Psi_{R}(M)
, we have

d_{0}Z_{1}\simeq d_{0}{\hat{\Psi}}_{R}(M)\mathcal{G}\simeq\Psi_{R}(M)\mathcal{G}
. Combining Lemma
4.13
, the pullback square defining
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
, and the evident way in which
d_{1}
interleaves the various functors labeled “
M^{(-)}
” and “
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}
”, we have a composite equivalence:

\begin{split}d_{1}(Z_{2}\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R})&\simeq(d_{1}Z_{2})\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}\simeq(d_{1}\circ\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}\circ M^{(-)}\circ\mathcal{F})\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}\\
&\simeq(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}\circ M^{(-)}\circ d_{1}\circ\mathcal{F})\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}\\
&\simeq(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{(-)}\circ M^{(-)}\circ\Re\circ\mathcal{G})\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}\\
&\simeq(\Phi_{R}(M)\mathcal{G})\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}\simeq(\Phi_{R}(M)\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R})\mathcal{G}.\end{split}

By Proposition
4.15
(see in particular Remark
4.16
), we then have an equivalence
d_{0}Z_{1}\simeq d_{1}(Z_{2}\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R})
.

Notation 5.1
.

We denote by
Z_{3}\colon\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}\to((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/})^{2\simeq}
the concatenation of the two functors
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

The
\infty
-functor
Z_{3}
factors through the localisation
\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathrm{GrCob}
.

Proof.

The image of
(A\to X)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{\mathcal{S}}
along
Z_{3}
is the object in
((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/})^{2\simeq}
given by the composite functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{A})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{X}\to M^{A})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{X})_{!}}

where the first arrow accounts for
Z_{1}
and the second two for
Z_{2}\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}
. This shows that
Z_{3}(A\to X)
is equivalent to the endofunctor
p(M^{X})_{!}p(M^{X})^{*}
of
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, which in turn is equivalent to
-\otimes_{R}(M^{X}\otimes R)
.

Without loss of generality, we may now consider an idle 1-morphism as in Remark
3.6
, and prove that it is inverted along
Z_{3}
.
This 1-morphism is sent along
Z_{3}
to the outer square of the following commutative diagram in
(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{2\simeq}
, considered as a morphism between vertical composites. Since the top and bottom rows are invertible functors, we obtain indeed an invertible 1-morphism in
((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/})^{2\simeq}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{M^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{B})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{A})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{Y}\to M^{B})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{A}\to M^{B})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{X}\to M^{A})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{Id}_{M^{A}})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{X}\to M^{A})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{X})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{X}\simeq M^{Y})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{X})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{Id}_{M^{X}})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{X})_{!}}

∎

5.2.
The lax comma categories of
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}

Recall that a colimit-preserving,
R
-linear endofunctor of
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is of the form
-\otimes_{R}\zeta
for some
\zeta\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, and that the space of
R
-linear natural transformations between
-\otimes_{R}\zeta
and
-\otimes_{R}\zeta^{\prime}
is equivalent to
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})
. In few words, the full
{(\infty,2)}
-subcategory of
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
spanned by the object
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is equivalent to the pointed
{(\infty,2)}
-category
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
obtained by delooping once the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, see Subsection
2.4
. The core groupoid of
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is equivalent to
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
, with basepoint denoted by
*
; the endomorphism
\infty
-category
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(*,*)
is equivalent to
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, and horizontal composition is given by tensor product of
R
-modules.

Definition 5.3
.

We define
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
as the wide symmetric monoidal
\infty
-subcategory of
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}
spanned by those morphisms that are sent along the target functor
d_{0}\colon((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}\to(\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})^{2\simeq}
to invertible morphisms. In symbols we have

((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}=((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}\times_{(B\mathrm{Mod}_{R})^{2\simeq}}\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R).

We define similarly
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}\subset((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}
.

Roughly speaking, objects in the
\infty
-category
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathrm{Pic}}
are
R
-modules; a morphism
\zeta\to\zeta^{\prime}
in
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
is the datum of an invertible
R
-module
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
and a morphism of
R
-modules
\zeta\otimes_{R}\zeta^{\prime\prime}\to\zeta^{\prime}
. The description of
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
is similar, except that a morphism
\zeta\to\zeta^{\prime}
is the datum of an invertible
R
-module
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
and a morphism
\zeta^{\prime}\to\zeta\otimes_{R}\zeta^{\prime\prime}
.

Remark 5.4
.

Barkan–Steinebrunner consider an alternative construction of the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
. They consider the action of
\mathrm{Pic}(R)
on
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
given by tensor product; there is an associated lax symmetric monoidal functor
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)\to\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
, and its colimit in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
, denoted
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/\mathrm{Pic}(R)
, is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category equivalent to our
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
.

As observed in the proof of Lemma
5.2
, the functor
Z_{3}
takes image inside the
\infty
-subcategory of

((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}|_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}})^{\text{l-lax}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/})^{2\simeq}
, comprising all objects and those 1-morphisms that are sent along
d_{0}
to an invertible 1-morphism; this
\infty
-subcategory corresponds precisely to
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
along the equivalence

((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}|_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}})^{\text{l-lax}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/})^{2\simeq}\simeq((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}.

We shall henceforth abuse notation and regard
Z_{3}
as a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functor
\mathrm{GrCob}\to((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
.

We also have a commutative square in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}})
, whose vertical maps are the target functors, and whose bottom equivalence is induced by the symmetric monoidal automorphism
(-)^{-1}\colon\mathrm{Pic}(R)\to\mathrm{Pic}(R)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{l-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}\big)^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

In particular we may pass to opposite categories and consider the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functor
Z_{3}^{\mathrm{op}}\colon\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}}\to((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
. The composite functor

d_{0}Z_{3}^{\mathrm{op}}\colon\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)

agrees with
(\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}
, and both functors are constant at
*
on spaces of objects.

Notation 5.5
.

We let
\mathrm{Seg}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}\subset s\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
denote the full
\infty
-subcategory spanned by (possibly non-complete)
\mathfrak{U}_{2}
-small Segal spaces, i.e. by functors
X\colon\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
satisfying the Segal condition: for all
p\geq 2
the canonical map of spaces
X([p])\to X([1])\times_{X([0])}X([1])\times_{X([0])}\dots\times_{X([0])}X([1])
is an equivalence.

Note that
\mathrm{Seg}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}\subset s\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
is closed under products, i.e. it is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-subcategory.

Definition 5.6
.

We denote by
\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Seg}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}})
the Segal space given by the following pullback of symmetric monoidal Segal spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]_{*}[0]^{*}}\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{[0]_{*}}}

where
[0]\colon*\to\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the functor picking the object
[0]
, and where
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
is considered as a constant simplicial space. The bottom right corner is informally the Segal space sending
[p]\mapsto(\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R))^{p+1}
, with face maps forgetting coordinates and degeneracy maps inserting
*\in\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
; its symmetric monoidal structure relies on the fact that both
[0]^{*}
and
[0]_{*}
are product preserving.

Intuitively,
\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)([p])\simeq\mathrm{Pic}(R)^{p}
; face maps are given by forgetting the first or last coordinate, or by multiplying two consecutive coordinates, using the group structure on
\mathrm{Pic}(R)
; and degeneracy maps are given by inserting
*\in\mathrm{Pic}(R)
.

By definition, for
X\in\mathrm{Seg}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
, a morphism
X\to\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
is the same as a morphism
X\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
together with a nullhomotopy of the induced map of spaces
X([0])\to\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
.
The previous discussion allows us to consider
(\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}
as a symmetric monoidal map of Segal spaces
\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)
, i.e. as an object in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Seg}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}_{/\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)})
. Here we model the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}}
as a
complete
Segal space.

Definition 5.7
.

We let
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Seg}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}_{/\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)})
denote the pullback of symmetric monoidal Segal spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The previous discussion also allows us to regard
Z_{3}^{\mathrm{op}}
as a morphism from
\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}}
to
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Seg}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}_{/\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)})
.

5.3.
Categories enriched in presheaf categories

In this subsection we make use of the theory of enriched
\infty
-categories as developed by Gepner–Haugseng
[
32
]
. For a (symmetric) monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, Gepner–Haugseng introduce an
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{cat}}(\mathcal{C})
of “categorical algebras in
\mathcal{C}
”, whose objects are pairs consisting of a space
S\in\mathcal{S}
and a map of generalised non-symmetric operads
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}_{S}\to\mathcal{C}
.
If
\mathcal{C}
is symmetric monoidal, then also
\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{cat}}(\mathcal{C})
admits a symmetric monoidal structures, so that it makes sense to talk of symmetric monoidal categorical algebras in
\mathcal{C}
.
A lax (symmetric) monoidal functor of (symmetric) monoidal
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
allows one to “transform” a categorical algebra in
\mathcal{C}
into a categorical algebra in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. Gepner–Haugseng also introduce an
\infty
-category of “
\mathcal{C}
-enriched
\infty
-categories”, denoted
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathcal{C}}
, which is a certain reflective full
\infty
-subcategory of
\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{cat}}(\mathcal{C})
spanned by “complete” objects; we shall content ourselves with categorical algebras here, and leave the discussion about completions to the interested reader. We will therefore also use terminology that clashes with
[
32
]
and refer to an object in
\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{cat}}(\mathcal{C})
as a “
\mathcal{C}
-enriched
\infty
-category”, and to a morphism in
\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{cat}}(\mathcal{C})
as a “
\mathcal{C}
-enriched
\infty
-functor”.

The identification
\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{cat}}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}})\simeq\mathrm{Seg}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
from
[
32
, Theorem 4.4.7]
is symmetric monoidal, as both
\infty
-categories carry the cartesian symmetric monoidal structure. Using this identification, we can get a symmetric monoidal identification
\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{cat}}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}_{/\mathrm{Pic}(R)})\simeq\mathrm{Seg}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}_{/\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}(R)}
. It follows that the symmetric monoidal map of Segal spaces
Z_{3}^{\mathrm{op}}\colon\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}}\to((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
corresponds to a symmetric monoidal
\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}_{/\mathrm{Pic}(R)}
-enriched functor, which for simplicity we still denote

Z_{3}^{\mathrm{op}}\colon\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}}\to((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathbb{B}\mathrm{Pic}}
.

We next recall that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}_{/\mathrm{Pic}(R)}\simeq\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Pic}(R),\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}})
in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{U}_{3}})
, see
[
64
, Corollary D]
. In this light, the
\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}_{/\mathrm{Pic}(R)}
-enriched category
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathrm{Pic}}
corresponds to the category
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, whose enrichment in
\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Pic}(R),\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}})
is given by first considering the self-enrichment of
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
and then by applying the following sequence of lax symmetric monoidal functors, where
\kappa<\mathfrak{U}_{1}
is a regular cardinal such that
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is
\kappa
-accessible (see Subsection
2.2
for background):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\kappa})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Pic}(R))\subset\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Pic}(R);\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!_{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{Pic}(R)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\kappa})^{*}}

We thus learn that
((\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})_{*/}^{\text{r-lax}})^{2\simeq}_{\mathrm{Pic}}
is in fact enriched in the smaller category
\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Pic}(R))=\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Pic}(R);\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{1}})
; the same holds also for
\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and hence
Z_{3}^{\mathrm{op}}
can be regarded as a
\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Pic}(R))
-enriched symmetric monoidal
\infty
-functor. By applying the symmetric monoidal left adjoints
(\mathrm{Pic}(R)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\kappa})_{!}
and
\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!^{\prime}_{\kappa}
to the previous composite of lax symmetric monoidal right adjoints, we finally obtain a
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
-enriched symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
(\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}
as the image of
\mathrm{GrCob}^{\mathrm{op}}\in\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{cat}}(\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Pic}(R)))
, and a
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
-enriched symmetric monoidal functor

\mathrm{GFT}_{M}\colon(\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}.

This concludes the proof of Theorem
A
, up to checking that
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}
indeed recovers the basic string operations (1)-(3) from Subsection
1.1
; this last step will be achieved in Section
6
. The proof of Corollary
A.1
from Theorem
A
is also immediate, using the fact that taking homotopy groups is a lax symmetric monoidal functor
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}=\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{Sp})\to\mathrm{Mod}_{\pi_{*}(R)}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Remark 5.8
.

The above composite
(\mathrm{Pic}(R)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\kappa})^{*}\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!_{\kappa}
, combined with the equivalence
\mathcal{S}_{/\mathrm{Pic}(R)}\simeq\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Pic}(R))
, gives rise to a lax symmetric monoidal functor
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\to\mathcal{S}_{/\mathrm{Pic}(R)}
; this functor associates with an
R
-module
\zeta
the moduli space of
R
-linear maps
\zeta^{\prime}\to\zeta
with
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Pic}(R)
; this is a space over
\mathrm{Pic}(R)
by forgetting the map and only retaining
\zeta^{\prime}
. The left adjoint functor
\mathcal{S}_{/\mathrm{Pic}(R)}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is the functor sending
\zeta\colon X\to\mathrm{Pic}(R)
to
\mathrm{colim}_{X}\zeta\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. In particular the morphism module from
(\emptyset\to Y)
to
(\emptyset\to X)
in
\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R}
is indeed
\mathrm{colim}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(Y,X)}\xi_{d}^{R}
as mentioned in Subsection
1.3
.

5.4.
Characteristic classes of homotopy automorphisms

We conclude the section by proving Corollary
A.2
. The functor
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}\colon\colon(\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
depends naturally on
M\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}
, so that we obtain a map of spaces

\mathrm{GFT}_{(-)}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{cat}}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}))\,((\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Mod}_{R}).

The crucial observation is now that composing with evaluation at the monoidal unit
\emptyset\in(\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R})^{\mathrm{op}}
gives rise to the constant map
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\simeq}
at
R\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
; indeed, for any
M\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}
, the mapping space
M^{\emptyset}
is canonically identified with
*\in\mathcal{S}
, and thus
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}(\emptyset)\simeq M^{\emptyset}\otimes R
is canonically identified with
R
.

We can thus compose
\mathrm{GFT}_{(-)}
with the evaluation at
\mathrm{GrCob}_{!}\xi_{d}^{R}(\emptyset,\emptyset)\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, i.e. the endomorphism object of
\emptyset
; we thus obtain a map of spaces

\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{R}(\mathrm{colim}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)}\xi_{d}^{R},\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{R}(R,R)),

where
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{R}(R,R)\simeq R\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is the internal endomorphism object of
R\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. The last datum is then equivalent to an
R
-linear pairing

(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}\otimes R)\otimes_{R}(\mathrm{colim}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Gr}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)}\xi_{d}^{R})\to R,

and the statement of Corollary
A.2
is obtained by restricting to the component in
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{Poin},d}^{R,+}
of a given oriented
R
-Poincaré duality space
M
.

6.
Recovering the basic string operations

Let
R
be an
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum and let

M
be a topological, closed,
R
-oriented
d
-dimensional manifold. In this subsection we conclude the proof of Theorem
A
, by identifying the basic operations (1)-(3) from Subsection
1.1
with the analogous operations provided by the functor
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}
.

6.1.
Recovering the operations (1) and (2)

We start with the basic operation (1). Given a map of spaces
f\colon Y\to X
, we consider it as the graph cobordism corresponding to the following diagram of spaces:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The functor
Z_{3}
from Notation
5.1
sends
f
to the following diagram in the
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
, giving a morphism in
((\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R})^{\text{l-lax}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}/})^{2\simeq}
between horizontal composites; here the left and right squares represent the contributions of
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}\otimes\ell\xi_{-d}^{R}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{Y})_{!}p(M^{Y})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{X})_{!}p(M^{X})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{M^{f}_{!}}}

Evaluating at
R\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
in the top left corner, we obtain that the image along
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}
of the previous graph cobordism is the following morphism in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
:

p(M^{X})_{!}p(M^{X})^{*}(R)\simeq p(M^{Y})_{!}M^{f}_{!}(M^{f})^{*}p(M^{Y})^{*}(R)\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{M^{f}_{!}}}p(M^{Y})_{!}p(M^{Y})^{*}(R).

After taking homotopy groups we obtain the map
H_{*}(M^{f};R)
as desired.

We next consider the basic operation (2). Given a decomposition
Y=X\sqcup*
, we obtain the graph cobordism corresponding to the following diagram of spaces:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}

The image of the previous along
Z_{3}
is the following diagram in
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Pr}^{L}_{R}
, in which we abbreviated
p=p(M)\colon M\to*
, and we denote by
[-d]\simeq-\otimes R[-d]
the
d
-fold desuspension functor:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{R}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{*}p^{*}\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{!}p^{*}[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{Y})_{!}p(M^{Y})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{p_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(M^{X})_{!}p(M^{X})^{*}}

Evaluating at
R
, we obtain that the image along
\mathrm{GFT}_{M}
of the previous graph cobordism is the following morphism in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, with target shifted by
-d
:

\begin{split}p(M^{X})_{!}p(M^{X})^{*}(R)&\simeq R\otimes p(M^{X})_{!}p(M^{X})^{*}(R)\xrightarrow{\eta_{p_{*}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}}\\
&\to p_{*}p^{*}(R)\otimes p(M^{X})_{!}p(M^{X})^{*}(R)\\
&\simeq p_{!}p^{*}(R)\otimes p(M^{X})_{!}p(M^{X})^{*}(R)[-d]\\
&\simeq p(M^{Y})_{!}p(M^{Y})^{*}(R)[-d].\end{split}

On homotopy groups, the previous composite agrees with the map
H_{*}(M^{X};R)\to H_{*+d}(M^{Y};R)
given by cross product with the fundamental class of
M
.

6.2.
The Chas–Sullivan product

We next recall the classical definition of the Chas-Sullivan product.
Let
X\in\mathrm{Top}
be a topological space, and let
Y
be the topological space obtained from
X
by attaching a 1-cell
I=[0,1]
to
X
, along a map
\psi\colon\partial I\to X
. Up to replacing
X
with the mapping cylinder of
\psi
, we may assume that
\psi
is a cofibration, and in particular we may regard
\partial I
as a subset of
X
; in this case
Y
is homotopy equivalent to the quotient
Y/I\cong X/\partial I
, and
M^{Y}
is homotopy equivalent to
M^{Y/I}\cong M^{X/\partial I}
.

Evaluation at
\partial I
gives a map
e\colon M^{X}\to M^{2}
, which is a fibre bundle; if
\Delta\colon M\hookrightarrow M^{2}
denotes the diagonal inclusion, we obtain a cartesian square in
\mathrm{Top}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{X/\partial I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

Let
U
be a neighbourhood of
\Delta(M)
in
M^{2}
that is
homeomorphic to the total space of the normal microbundle
4
4

4

For simplicity we will not distinguish between microbundles and topological
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-bundles.

N_{\Delta}M\to M
. Let
V=e^{-1}(U)
be the corresponding neighbourhood of
M^{X/\partial I}
in
M^{X}
. Then the quotient
M^{2}/(M^{2}\setminus U)
is homeomorphic to the Thom space
\mathrm{Th}(N_{\Delta}M\to M)
; similarly,
V
is homeomorphic to the total space of the microbundle
e_{1}^{*}(N_{\Delta}M)\to M^{X/\partial I}
, and
M^{X}/(M^{X}\setminus V)
is homeomorphic to the Thom space
\mathrm{Th}(e_{1}^{*}(N_{\Delta}M)\to M^{X/\partial I})
.

Recall from Definition
2.9
that an
R
-orientation on
M
is an isomorphism
\omega_{M}^{R}\simeq p^{*}(R[-d])
, where
p=p(M)\colon M\to*
is the terminal map. An orientation on
M
yields an
R
-orientation on
M^{2}
, as product of the orientations of the two factors: more precisely, we have a canonical identification
\omega_{M^{2}}^{R}\simeq p_{1}^{*}(\omega_{M}^{R})\otimes p_{2}^{*}(\omega_{M}^{R})
, where
p_{1},p_{2}\colon M^{2}\to M
are the two projections, and we may identify the latter expression with
(p^{2})^{*}(R[-d])\otimes(p^{2})^{*}(R[-d])\simeq(p^{2})^{*}(R[-2d])

5
5

5

The reader will notice that the last identification
R[-d]\otimes R[-d]\simeq R[-2d]
also depends on a choice of
order
of the two factors, i.e. it can be sensitive to swapping the two factors; for instance this occurs when
R=\mathbb{Z}
and
d
is odd. This is reflected in the generic non-triviality of the coefficient systems
\xi_{d}^{R}
.
.
The normal microbundle
N_{\Delta}M
, which is a topological
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-bundle over
M
, gives rise to a fibration of pointed spaces on
M
, with fibre the pointed space
S^{d}
, by taking one-point compactifications fibrewise; by further taking suspension spectra fibrewise we obtain a spherical fibration
n_{\Delta}M\in\mathrm{Sp}^{M}
, with fibres isomorphic to
\mathbb{S}[d]
. We have moreover a canonical identification

\Delta^{*}(\omega_{M^{2}}^{\mathbb{S}})\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}n_{\Delta}M\simeq\omega_{M}^{\mathbb{S}},

coming from the isomorphism of microbundles
\Delta^{*}(tM^{2})\cong TM\oplus N_{\Delta}(M)
and the identifications
\omega_{M}^{\mathbb{S}}\simeq tM^{-1}
and
\omega_{M^{2}}^{\mathbb{S}}\simeq(tM^{2})^{-1}
. Here
tM
and
tM^{2}
denote the tangent microbundles.

The
R
-orientations on
M
and
M^{2}
, together with the isomorphism
n_{\Delta}M\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}R\simeq\omega_{M}^{R}\otimes_{R}\Delta^{*}(\omega_{M^{2}}^{R})^{-1}
, give an equivalence
n_{\Delta}M\simeq p^{*}(R[d])
; by pullback along
e_{1}
we also obtain an equivalence
e_{1}^{*}(n_{\Delta}M)\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}R\simeq p(M^{X/\partial I})^{*}(R[d])
.

We can then define an operation
H_{*}(M^{X};R)\to H_{*-d}(M^{W};R)
, which we shall refer to as the
general Chas–Sullivan operation
, as the composite

\begin{split}&H_{*}(M^{X};R)\to H_{*}(M^{X},M^{X}\setminus V;R)\cong\tilde{H}_{*}(\mathrm{Th}(e_{1}^{*}(N_{\Delta}M)\to M^{X/\partial I});R)\cong\\
&H_{*}(M^{X/\partial I};e_{1}^{*}(n\Delta(M))\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}R)\cong H_{*}(M^{X/\partial I};R[d])\cong H_{*-d}(M^{Y};R).\end{split}

Here the isomorphism connecting the two lines is the Thom isomorphism, identifying the homology of a Thom space with the homology of the base twisted in the associated invertible parametrised
R
-module.

The mainly studied case of the previous string operation is the Chas-Sullivan product. We let
X
be the disjoint union
S^{1}\sqcup S^{1}
, and we let
\psi
send the two points of
\partial I
to two points lying on the two different copies of
S^{1}
. We then have
Y\simeq S^{1}\vee S^{1}
, and we obtain a map
H_{*}(LM\times LM;R)\to H_{*-d}(M^{S^{1}\vee S^{1}};R)
. We can further compose this with the type (1) operation
H_{*-d}(M^{S^{1}\vee S^{1}};R)\to H_{*-d}(LM;R)
induced by the pinch map
S^{1}\to S^{1}\vee S^{1}
; the resulting composite
H_{*}(LM\times LM;R)\to H_{*-d}(LM;R)
is the Chas-Sullivan product
[
14
]
.

To interpret the above construction in terms of parametrised
R
-modules,
define the topological spaces
\partial U:=U\setminus\Delta(M)
and
\partial V:=V\setminus\tilde{\Delta}(M^{X/\partial I})=e^{-1}(\partial U)
, and
consider the following commutative cube in
\mathcal{S}
, whose top and bottom face are pushouts, and whose vertical faces are pullbacks:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\simeq M^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{X}\setminus V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\simeq M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}\setminus U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

Then the above composite giving the general Chas–Sullivan string operation at the level of homology can be identified with the evaluation at
R
of the natural transformation obtained from the top composite in the following diagram, after precomposition by
p(M^{X})^{*}
and postcomposition by
(p^{2})_{!}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{!}\mathrm{cof}(\epsilon_{\tilde{c}_{!}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{!}\tilde{\Delta}_{!}\mathrm{cof}(\epsilon_{\tilde{a}_{!}})\tilde{\Delta}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{!}\tilde{\Delta}_{!}\tilde{\Delta}^{*}[d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cof}(\epsilon_{c_{!}})e_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{!}\mathrm{cof}(\epsilon_{a_{!}})\Delta^{*}e_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{!}\Delta^{*}e_{!}[d].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TI}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TI}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC}

In the previous diagram we use Notation
2.7
, and the arrows labeled “
TI
